iii 
 
PREFACE 
 
I, the Chairman of the Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health 
and Family Welfare, having been authorized by the Committ ee to present the Report on its 
behalf, present this  168th Report of the Committee on Action Taken by the Government on the 
Recommendations/ Observations contained in the 165 th Report on Demands for Grants 2025-26 
(DEMAND NO. 4) of the Ministry of Ayush. 
2. The 165th Report of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health 
and Family Welfare was presented to Rajya Sabha on 12 th March 2025 and laid on the Table of 
Lok Sabha on 12 th March 2025. The Action Taken Notes of the Government on th e 
recommendations contained in the Report was received from the Department in the month of 
June 2025.   
3.  The Committee made a total of 83 recommendations/observations in the 165 th Report, out 
of which 30 recommendations have been accepted by the Committ ee and have been categorized 
under Chapter -I. There is 1 recommendation that the Committee does not desire to pursue in 
view of the Department‘s replies that have been categorized under Chapter -II. There are 42 
recommendations/ observations, in respect of which replies of the Department have not been 
accepted by the Committee and the Committee has made further recommendations thereon and 
have been categorized under Chapter -III. While 10 recommendations/observations in respect of 
which final replies of the Ministry have not been received, have been categorized under Chapter - 
IV. 
4. The Committee, in its meeting held on 24th September, 2025, considered the Draft Report 
and adopted the same. 
 
 
NEW DELHI                        PROF. RAM GOPAL YADAV 
24th September, 2025          CHAIRMAN 
Ashwin, 1947 (Saka)                   Department-related Parliamentary Standing 
                  Committee on Health and Family Welfare

REPORT

Recommendations/ Observations contained in the 165 th Report Demands for Grants 2025 - 2026 
(Demand No. 4) about Ministry of Ayush.

in respect of the recommendations contained in the Report. They have been categorized as 
follows:

1 
 
CHAPTER- I 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS WHICH HAVE BEEN ACCEPTED BY THE 
MINISTRY 
 
FUNCTIONAL AREAS OF THE MINISTRY 
 
1.1. The Committee takes into account the thematic areas of Ayush activities and the institutional 
framework to achieve the objectives of those activities. The Committee desires that each and 
every institution must strive towards achievin g the Mission Objective of the Ministry of Ayush 
within the specified time framework. The Committee further observes that the broad thematic 
area of Ayush activities is the key to accomplish the mission objectives of the Ministry i.e., 
improve access to hi gh quality and affordable Ayurveda, Unani, Siddha and Sowa -Rigpa 
(AUS&SR) & Homeopathy medical education, availability of adequate and high quality 
AUS&SR & Homeopathy medical professionals in all parts of the country, promote equitable and 
universal healt hcare that encourages community health perspective. The thematic area of 
Quality research in Ayush is undertaken through pioneer research councils under MoA such as 
CCRAS, CCRUM, CCRS, CCRH and CCRYN. The Committee, however, feels that in order to 
address emerging challenges of healthcare, there is a need for functional integration of Ayurveda 
and conventional medicine with its affordable and evidence based use to boost Ayurveda in the 
paradigm of contemporary scientific, technological and medical parameters. 
(Para 1.3.3 of 165th Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.1.1 AEP – III Section 
 NCH 
The measures to integrate Homeopathy with conventional medicine has been taken by 
prescribing a comprehensives content about knowledge of Medicine, psychology, emergency and 
Modern Pharmacology in the under graduate Curriculum by regulation. 
Also, to meet with the challenges of the constantly changing and evolving field of medicine with 
new technologies, practices, and innovations, the Commission has decided to introduce 
Continuing M edical Education (CMEs) essentially for upgradation registered practitioners of 
Homoeopathy by regulation. For the same the Autonomous Board of Ethics and Registration for 
Homoeopathy (BERH) of the Commission has placed the draft amendment on website to seek for 
comments/opinion/advice/suggestions/ objection for from the stake holders and general public. 
L&P Section (PCIM&H)  
 Pharmacopoeial Publications:  The commission develops and amend the 
Pharmacopoeias and Formularies of Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani and Homo eopathy System 
of Medicines at regular intervals to ascertain the identity, purity and strength of ASU&H 
medicines. Further, the development of quality standards in respect of Sowa -Rigpa 

2 
 
medicines are under progress.   
 Act as a Central Drug cum Testing Laboratory 
PCIM&H also act as an appellate drug testing laboratory receives the samples from Govt. 
agencies as per Drugs & Cosmetics Act & Rules there under for ascertaining their quality. 
 Capacity Building Training Programs:  PCIM&H regularly conducts capacit y-building 
training sessions for stakeholders, including drug regulators, enforcement authorities, and 
technical staff from ASU&H drug testing laboratories. 
 PCIM&H also entered in MoU with National and International bodies to promote the 
research and development in area of traditional medicines.  
Scheme Section 
Under Ayurgyan Scheme, a new component namely ABIHR has been added from FY 2023 -24 
with the aim to integrate Ayurveda and Conventional Medicine by supporting high end research 
in the field of Ayush.   Further, under Centre of Excellence component of AyurswasthyaYojana, 
09 projects were supported to National reputed Institutes/Organizations to integrate the Ayurveda 
with Modern Medicine.

 CCRAS 
CCRAS has undertaken various research studies to examine the benefits and feasibility of 
integration of Ayurveda with modern system of medicine. 

4 
 
The details are as follows: 
1. Operational study to explore the feasibility of integrating Ayurveda with modern system of 
medicine in a tertiary care hospital (Safdarjung Hospital New Delhi) for the management of 
Osteoarthritis (Knee)- Completed 
2. Feasibility of introducing the Indian  System of Medicine (Ayurveda) in the National 
Reproductive and Child Health services at the Primary Health Care (PHC) level‖ in Himachal 
Pradesh- Completed  
3. Integration of AYUSH systems in the National Programme for Prevention and Control of 
Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases &amp; Stroke (NPCDCS)- Completed 
4. Feasibility of introducing Ayurveda Intervention in Reproductive and Child Health 
(RCH) in PHCs of the Selected district (Gadchiroli) of Maharashtra (Effectiveness of Ayurvedic 
intervention for Ante -Natal care (GarbhiniParicharya) at Primary Health Care level: A Multi 
Centre Operational Study)- Completed  
Further, Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR) and Central Council for Research in 
Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS) under Ministry of Ayush  has taken and initiative to set up Ayush -
ICMR Advanced Centre for Integrative Health Research (AI -ACIHR), at AIIMS to conduct 
research on identified areas focusing on integrative healthcare under Extra Mural research 
Scheme   of ICMR . Under this program four research areas in four AIIMS have been identified, 
which are as follows: 
i. AIIMS Delhi: 
a) Advanced Centre for Integrative Health Research in Gastro-intestinal Disorders 
b) Advanced Centre for Integrative Health Research in Women and Child Health 
ii. AIIMS- Jodhpur: Advanced Centre for Integrative Health Research in Geriatric Health  
iii. AIIMS Nagpur: Advanced Centre for Integrative Health Research in Cancer Care  
iv. AIIMS Rishikesh: Advanced Centre for Integrative Health Research in Geriatric Health. 
 AIIA 
The integration of All India Institute of Ayurveda (AIIA)  with premier institutions such as 
AIIMS Delhi, Institute of Liver and Biliary Sciences (ILBS), CSIR -Institute of Genomics and 
Integrative Biology (IGIB), and Indian Institutes of Technology (IIT)-Delhi, alongside 
establishment of satellite centres of AIIA at Safdarjung Hospital and CAIO at NCI -AIIMS 
Jhajjar—represents a strategic convergence of traditional wisdom and modern science to address 
emerging healthcare challenges. This multidiscipli nary partnership aims to establish a functional 
model of integrative medicine that combines the strengths of Ayurveda and conventional 
biomedicine through joint research, clinical trials, advanced diagnostics, and technology -driven 
innovations. By fosterin g evidence -based, affordable, and patient -centric healthcare solutions, 
this initiative seeks to position Ayurveda within the contemporary scientific paradigm, enhancing 
its global credibility and utility in managing complex diseases such as cancer, liver disorders, 
metabolic syndromes, and chronic inflammatory conditions. 

5 
 
 NIA 
The Institute produces 125 graduates, 130 post -graduates, 12 post -graduates in inter -disciplines, 
28 post-doctoral fellows, 30 diploma holders and 30 trained panchakarma technicians e very year.  
The Institute imparts high quality of affordable education in the field of Ayurveda at all levels.  
The Institute produces more than 200 varieties of quality drugs for dispensing to its patients in 
IPD, OPD and also for research purposes.  The Institute also undertakes research activities of 
various topics by its teachers as well as by undertaking research projects with Government and 
non-governmental organizations including industries and so far 35 MoUs are in progress. 
 NEIAH 
The Institute has actively been integrating AYUSH  with modern medicines through various 
research initiatives and institutional collaborations. Here are some notable development on 
research activities: 
Ongoing Research Project entitle: The title of the project is  ―Promoting the Ayurveda 
interventions for Iron Deficiency Anemia (IDA) among tribal females (18 to 45 year of age)  in 
Mawpat and Mylliem Block of East Khasi Hills district of Meghalaya under component of 
AyurswasthyaYojana. 
An ongoing project of NEIAH collaborating with Central Council for Research in Homoeopathy 
(CCRH) for a Research project in the Implementation of Homoeopathy for Menstrual Health of 
Adolescent School Going Girl‘s - A Public Health Research Program at East Khasi Hill District, 
Meghalaya. 
 Moreover, North Eastern Institute of Ayurveda and Homeopathy, (NEIAH) Shillong has 
integrated modern practices in physiotherapy, dentistry and dietary etc. in its hospital to provide a 
comprehensive health care for patients' benefit.  
Moreover, laboratory and radiol ogy procedures are routinely conducted to supplement the 
Ayurvedic diagnosis of patients.  
NEIAH has also ensured high quality and affordable Ayush facilities through its two hospitals 
and a peripheral OPD at Smit Village.  
 NISR 
Along with Ayurveda Sowa-Rigpa systems may also include to implement the program. 
Siddha & Unani Section 
 CCRS 
 The Central Council for Research in Siddha (CCRS), an apex organization for the 
formulation, co-ordination, development, and promotion of research on scientific lines in 
Siddha has research program to explore avenues for research and Development of safe, 
effective Siddha medicines and therapeutic techniquesfor the treatment of diseases of 
national and global importance.  
 The Council is engaged in multifaceted research activit ies. Clinical research, pre -clinical 
Pharmacological / Toxicological studies, Open label trials, multi -centric Randomized 

6 
 
Controlled Trials (RCT‘s) are being conducted for acute and chronic diseases. 
Strengthening Research and Innovation through aligning S iddha Research with diseases 
of National priorities through IMR schemes 
 CCRS has been granting Research project grants to UG, PG siddha students to inculcate 
the scientific temper in the young minds. 
 CCRS organises oral and poster scientific competitions f or the medical students to 
motivate and impart scientific knowledge. In addition to this workshop, training 
programmes, symposium are being organised by CCRS for the Siddha medical students/ 
teachers to develop quality education and professionals  
 To stren gthen research in Siddha among students and researchers, the following have 
been initiated:  
1. Student Academic Initiative for Research in Siddha (STAIRS) 
2. CCRS Post-Doctoral Fellowship Scheme (PDF)  
3. Ph.DScheme for Siddha and inter-disciplinary life sciences 
4. Siddha Training Accreditation Board (STAB)  
 CCRS has taken initiatives for integrating Siddha practices with the mainstream 
healthcare system. Integration of Siddha therapies in primary healthcare systems through 
collaborative activities. The integration o f the Siddha system with modern medicine 
through its holistic wellness and comprehensive treatment will provide new horizons of 
health care.  
 Steps have been initiated for integration with Allopathic care to offer synergistic care. 
Integration with Mainstr eam Healthcare ensuring its availability and accessibility to the 
Public. Integrated OPD for Diabetes in 3 peripheral Institutes / Units have been started at

 Integrative Health Research throug h developing evidence-based integrative practices that 
combine traditional Ayush knowledge with modern scientific research. Integration of 
Siddha therapies in primary healthcare systems through collaborative activities through 
the institutions like AIIMS R ishikesh (for Cancer Management, Reproductive & 
Childcare and Regenerative Medicine), Adyar Cancer institute, Chennai (for Cancer 
Management) have been initiated by CCRS. 
 Hence, to address emerging challenges of healthcare, there is a need for functional 
integration of Siddha Medicine and Conventional medicine with its affordable and 
evidence based use to boost Siddha System of Medicine in the paradigm of contemporary 
scientific, technological and medical parameters.

Homoeopathy Section 
In agreement with the recommendations of the committee that in order to address emerging 
challenges of healthcare, there is a need for functional integration of homoeopathy and 
conventional medicine with its affordable and evidence based use to boost homoeopathy in the 
paradigm of contemporary scientific, technological and medical parameters. 
In this  regard, CCRH has undertaken studies in collaboration with various institutes where 
homoeopathy has been used as adjunct/integration with standard care in diseases like substance 
abuse, diabetes, multimorbidity, tuberculosis or as comparator with standard care. 
Studies to assess the safety and efficacy of homoeopathic medicines on preclinical models like 
laboratory /animal based studies; mechanistic models/ signalling pathways/nanoscience domain 
have been undertaken in integrative manner with institutes of allied sciences. 
 
PROPOSED INITIATIVES TO BE TAKEN DURING THE FINANCIAL YEAR 2025-26 
 
1.2 The Committee appreciates the milestones achieved by the Ministry in year 2023 -24 & 2024-
25 and takes note of initiatives proposed for the coming FY 2025 -26. Given th e operational 
efficiency, professional pursuit and proper & adequate institutional support for research 
councils, educational institutes, regulatory bodies, etc., the Committee observes that the Ayush 
system is striving towards establishing itself as an alternative system of medicine. The Committee 
desires that the Ministry should make concerted efforts for optimal utilization of the potential of 
available AYUSH resources and establish the Ayush system within a pluralistic system of 
Integrative healthcare a nd re -emphasizing the need for integrating AYUSH in the National 
Health Mission/National Health Priorities, research and education in convergence with the 
objectives of National Health Policy (NHP) 2017. The Committee recommends the Ministry to 
lay focus o n the implementation of its schemes, maintaining high standards of education and 
research and strengthen its monitoring mechanism vide its institutional statutory and regulatory 
bodies to have Always Better Control (ABC) over quality and safety of AYUSH dr ugs. The 
Committee appreciates the new initiatives for the coming year and is of the view that Ayurveda 
and Yoga should reach every household for a Healthy and Fit India.

Ayurveda Section 
 ITRA 
ITRA is constantly working in line with National policies and will keep working in convergence 
with the objectives of National Health Policy (NHP) 2017.  PoshanaPakhwada, PoshanaMaaha, 
TB elimination program, International Yoga Day, World Meditation Day, B reast feeding week 
celebration etc are the national programs which ITRA is celebrating to achieve the national 
Goals. 
 
 CCRAS 
In pursuit of establishing Ayurveda as a scientifically validated system within the broader 
framework of integrative healthcare, th e Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences 
(CCRAS) has undertaken a wide range of clinical, pre -clinical, and public health research 
initiatives. These include studies on the feasibility, safety, and therapeutic benefits of integrative 
protocols combining Ayurveda with modern systems of medicine. The Council is also engaged in 
generating evidence to support the mainstreaming of Ayurveda within national health programs 

9 
 
and disease management strategies.  In a significant step towards fostering conve rgence between 
traditional and modern systems of medicine, the Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR) 
and CCRAS have collaboratively initiated the establishment of the Ayush -ICMR Advanced 
Centre for Integrative Health Research (AI -ACIHR) at AIIMS. This centre is mandated to 
undertake high -impact, transdisciplinary research in identified priority areas of integrative 
healthcare under the Extra-Mural Research Scheme of ICMR. The AI -ACIHR is envisioned as a 
model of institutional collaboration that will fac ilitate scientific validation and policy integration 
of AYUSH interventions.  To ensure scientific rigor and regulatory compliance, all CCRAS -
funded research proposals undergo structured appraisal through the Internal Scrutiny Committee 
(ISC), Project Evalu ation and Monitoring Committee (PEMC), and the Scientific Advisory 
Group (SAG) and also reviewed by Institutional Ethics Committees and Data and Safety 
Monitoring Boards, as appropriate. These mechanisms ensure that research proposals align with 
Good Clinical Practice (GCP) guidelines and the ICMR National Guidelines for Biomedical and 
Health Research involving Human Participants. Furthermore, a robust monitoring framework has 
been instituted wherein projects are routinely monitored centrally by concerned t eam at CCRAS. 
In addition, periodic field visits and independent audits by monitoring teams are carried out to 
verify adherence to protocols, timelines, and quality benchmarks.  CCRAS has also undertaken a 
project to explore the impact of Ayurveda -based lif estyle, including Yoga and other 
interventionsin the community across five cities of India, with a sample size of 1200. 
 
 AIIA 
The International Day of Yoga and Ayurveda Day are observed every year by the institute and as 
per the directions received from Mi nistry from time to time. The AIIA was made nodal agency 
for 09th International Ayurveda Day observed on 29 th Oct., 2025.   A total of 4816 activities, 18 
ideas and 55,05,829 supported Ayurveda registered in the website 
https://ayurvedaday.in/index.php dedicatedly made to display all activities of Ayurveda day. 
These activities collectively reached millions, promoting Ayurveda‘s role in holistic health and 
wellness. AIIA is running two campuses in Delhi and Goa  covering UG, PG and PhD 
programmes in Ayurveda duly affiliated by the Universities of Delhi and Goa.  Both campuses 
follow the r ules and regulations of NCISM. The trans -disciplinary researches are conducted 
which are firstly approved by the various intern al research committees including external experts 
of Ayush and modern medicine.  Similarly, all clinical research studies are registered under 
Clinical Trial Registry India (CTRI).

 CCRUM

experts as per Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), Ayush and Good Clinical Practice 
(GCP) guidelines. The Protocols are presented before the respective research -subcommittees and 
Scientific Advisory Committee (SAC). Ethical Issues are seen by the IEC and onsite monitoring 
is done by Institutional Multi Disciplinary Research Advisory Committee (IMRAC) regularly.

AYURSWASTHAY YOJANA 
 
1.9 The Committee expresses its concern over the variations in the budget allocation at BE and 
RE stage during 2024 -25 in regard to AyurswasthyaYojana. The Committee is of the  view that 
since the AyurswasthyaYojana aims to promote Ayush intervention for community health care for 
achieving Sustainable Development Goal 2 (SDG-2) and Sustainable Development Goal 3 (SDG-
3) and documentation of the efficacy of the Ayush System. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Ministry to implement the Yojana on priority basis with added impetus. The 
Committee desires that the Ministry should engage adequate manpower for successful 
implementation of the scheme to achieve its cherished objecti ves. The Committee observes with 
concern that the CoE scheme has suffered in the year 2024 -25, therefore the Committee 
recommends the Ministry to create awareness among stakeholders, establishing a digital 
platform for application entry, examinations and reviews of proposals, and ensuring smooth fund 
flow as per government guidelines. The Committee believes that regular discussions with 
organizations, diligent monitoring in this regard will be beneficial for the propagation and 
popularization of the Yojana.  The Committee expects the Ministry to strictly follow the Annual 
action plan and OOMF in respect of AyurswasthyaYojana so that targets set therein viz. 
sustainable development goals 2 & 3 are achieved within set timeframe. 
 
(Para 3.8.3 ibid)

CONSERVATION, DEVELOPMENT AND SUSTAINABLE MANAGEMENT OF

1.11 The Committee notes the mission objective of „Conservation, Development and Sustainable 
Management of Medicinal Plants‟. The Committee is of the considered view that Ministry 
should continue to take concrete steps to ensure Quality Assurance, Supply chain and 
creating/optimizing market linkages and value addition in the scheme for Conservation, 
Development and Sustainable Management of Medic inal Plants. There is already a provision 
for supporting the projects on quality assurance for maintaining good standards while 
collecting, cultivating and post -harvest handling of the raw material. In this regard, the 
Committee desires that the Ministry s hould ensure effective implementation of Voluntary 
Certification Schemes for Medicinal Plants produce (VCSMPP) to encourage Good 
Agricultural Practice (GAP) and Good Field Collection Practices (GFCP) in medicinal plants 
and enhance quality and safety of th ese plants. Although, the Committee appreciates the 
achievements of NMPB in field of conservation, development and sustainable management of 
medicinal plants, however, the Committee recommends the Ministry to give fresh further 
impetus to promote India on Global Medical Tourism encouraging foreign nationals to seek an 
alternative mode of treatment through Ayush. 
(Para 3.10.6 ibid)

recommends NCH to make SWOT Analysis of working and take remedial measures. Homeopathy 
should be promoted for equitable and universal healthcare on ground level as on date, however, 
to increase faith of the public in Homeopathy the Commission should take necessary steps to 
assess the efficacy of Homeopathy treatment. The Committee also recommends NCH to impr ove

to conduct homoeopathic medical camps at their hospitals and surrounding areas. The concept of 
peripheral homoeopathic OPDs has been introduced to promote and access the efficacy of 
homoeopathic treatment.  The medical  field is constantly changing and evolving with new 
technologies, practices, and innovations. The Board of Ethics and Registration for Homoeopathy 
drafted Continuing Medical Education (CMEs) for Homoeopathic practitioners for updating of 
knowledge and acco rdingly placed the draft amendment on the website of NCH for seeking

21

level to promote young researchers and it has taken various new initiatives like early clinical

psychology and yoga.

modern understanding with a regular update.

modern advancement, research and technology in homoeopathy (SMART -Hom) is included in 
regulation.

and teachers in association with scientist of Central Council for Research in Homoeopathy.

about Ayush systems of medicine, run peripheral OPDs within the radius o f 25 kms from the 
collegiate hospital and conduct medical camps. 
CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN AYURVEDIC SCIENCES 
 
1.14 The Committee appreciates the steps taken by CCRAS for internal revenue generation. The 
Committee takes note of the revenue generated through user charges collection from patients 
availing healthcare service in OPDs, IPDs etc and expects that the charges are kept affordable 
for the general public. The Committee also suggests that strong proposals be made towards 
getting CSR funding and i ts rationale and optimal utilization. Corporate Social Responsibility 
(CSR) presents a significant opportunity to bridge funding gaps and support innovative 
initiatives in the Ayush sector. By engaging with corporates and aligning projects with their 
CSR o bjectives, substantial financial and infrastructural support can be secured. The role of 
CSR extends beyond mere funding on CSR; it fosters strategic partnerships, enhances 
community outreach, and promotes sustainable development. Effective utilization of CSR funds 
can help in capacity building, infrastructure enhancement, research, and large -scale public 
health interventions, ensuring a greater impact on society. 
(Para 3.14.5 ibid) 
 
 

22 
 
Action Taken  
1.14.1 CCRAS has drafted Guidelines for utilization of CSR Funds which have been duly 
approved by the CCRAS Executive Committee (EC) and the Chairman (MoS I/c), Governing 
Body. CCRAS is proactively reaching out to various industries, organizations, and PSUs for CSR 
funding and has received a positive response from several entities. 
 
1.15 The Committee notes that nearly 271 research projects have been completed during the last 
5 years. The Committee observes that it is essential to document findings in a comprehensive 
final report and publish results in peer -reviewed journals and conferences to disseminate 
knowledge. Practical applications should be identified, with collaborations established to 
implement solutions. Post -project evaluations and stakeholder feedback help assess the 
research's impact and areas for improvement. Intellectual property opportunities, such as patents 
and commercialization, should be explored while ensuring legal compliance. Research data must 
be securely archived and shared through openaccess platforms wherever appropriate. Future 
research d irections should be identified, leveraging collaborative opportunities and funding 
sources to be rationally utilized in development of scientific evidence in Ayurvedic principle 
drugs therapies by way of integrating ancient wisdom with modern technology th rough scientific 
innovation pertaining to diagnostics, prevention, promoting as well as treatment method and 
translating all into public health system. The Committee recommends to the Ministry to ensure 
compliance with ethical, regulatory, and funding requ irements, including proper 
acknowledgments that are crucial for maintaining accountability and credibility in Ayurvedic 
System of Medicine 
(Para 3.14.6 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.15.1 The Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences systematically documen ts the 
outcomes of all completed research projects and to ensure wider dissemination and stakeholder 
engagement, research findings are regularly published in peer -reviewed journals, made available 
in the Ayush Research Portal and presented at national and international conferences, and 
compiled in CCRAS News bulletins.  Post-project evaluations are routinely conducted to assess 
the translational potential, societal relevance, and scientific merit of completed projects. 
Feedback from stakeholders,is factored into this review. Where applicable, findings with 
potential for innovation and application are forwarded to the Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) 
Cell of CCRAS for further assessment. This cell is mandated to explore patentability, facilitate 
filing processes, and guide commercialization strategies, in full compliance with national legal 
and regulatory frameworks. Research data generated by is securely archived in project-specific e-
formats with controlled access. Efforts are underway to enhance data shari ng through open -
access platforms, ensuring transparency.  CCRAS actively identifies and prioritizes future 
research directions that align with national health priorities and emerging public health needs. 

23 
 
The Council is committed to leveraging collaborative opportunities with academic institutions, 
research organizations, and industry partners to pool expertise and resources.CCRAS ensures full 
adherence to ethical norms, regulatory frameworks, and funding protocols in all research 
activities. Each project is conducted following the Good Clinical Practice (GCP) guidelines and 
the ICMR National Ethical Guidelines for Biomedical and Health Research involving Human 
Participants (2017). Institutional Ethics Committees (IECs) oversee approvals and periodic 
review. P roper acknowledgment of financial and institutional support is emphasized in all 
publications and outputs to maintain transparency, accountability, and scientific integrity in the 
Ayurvedic system of medicine.

NATIONAL INSTITUTE OF HOMOEOPATHY 
1.19 The Committee has taken note of the financial and physical performance of NIH. The 
Committee observes that variation in budgetary allocation during last three years is due to 
construction, renovation a nd upgrading of facilities. The Committee, however, notes with 
concern that under-utilization of funds are due to unfilled posts, pending bills for outsourced staff 
and partial functioning of NIH, Narela which casts aspersion on efficient working of NIH. T he 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommend for optimal utilization of allocated funds to avoid time 
and cost overruns over undertaken projects. The Committee observes that the patient footfall in 
Narelacentre has not been upto the expected level since its inception. The Committee accordingly 
suggests the Ministry to carry out awareness programmes in the NCT Region and adjoining 

25 
 
states to promote this centre and encouraging the patient to get quality homeopathy treatment 
from this centre. 
(Para 3.19.8 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.19.1 NIH has undertaken the following measures to fill up the vacant posts: 
i. The recruitment process for regular posts for NIH, Kolkata and Delhi is at its final stage, 
and till date, all Gr. B & C incumbents and few Gr. A incumbents have j oined the 
Institute. 
ii. Offer letters have been issued to the candidates selected for Gr. 'A' posts at NIH, Delhi.  
iii. Interviews for the posts of Joint Director (Administration) and Administrative Officer, 
NIH, Delhi (on deputation basis), have been conducted o n 06.05.2025 at the Ministry of 
Ayush. 
iv. Presently, NIH, Delhi employs 127 outsourced manpower through two agencies and 16 
contractual employees. Further, Walk -in interviews have been conducted for 24 
categories of contractual positions and 23 offer letters have been issued. 
v. NIH is actively taking steps to fill up the remaining vacant posts.  
Patient Care Service at NIH, Delhi: 
The National Institute of Homoeopathy (NIH), Delhi, has experienced a consistent increase in 
patient footfall since its inception, su ccessfully providing quality Homoeopathic treatment to all 
visiting individuals. From June 2024 to May 2025, a total of 116,207 patients sought treatment at 
NIH, Delhi, comprising 41,978 new patients and 74,229 follow-up patients. 
In addition to its clinic al services, NIH, Delhi, has actively engaged in community outreach. The 
institute has conducted numerous awareness programs for students and staff at institutions such 
as PM Shri KendriyaVidyalaya, Narela, Delhi, and Govt. Sarvodaya Bal Vidyalaya, Lampur 
Road, Bankner. Furthermore, many schools, NGOs, and other organizations have visited NIH, 
Delhi, to gain a comprehensive understanding of Homoeopathic treatment options. 
Committed to a holistic approach to patient well -being, the Institute is operating a d edicated 
Yoga and Physiotherapy Centre on its campus. To ensure equitable access to Homoeopathic care, 
NIH, Delhi, also runs specialized Outpatient Departments (OPDs) tailored for senior citizens and 
Armed Forces personnel. 
 
NATIONAL AYUSH MISSION 

26 
 
1.20 The Committee is given to understand that NAM is centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS) with 
Centre and State share in ratio of 60:40. Grant-in-aid is released to those States/Uts who have in 
compliance with the guidelines of Ministry of Finance, Department of Exp enditure as per their 
requirements. The Committee notes with appreciation that 12273 AyushArogyaMandirs have 
been operationalised, however, the remaining 227 AAMs out of target of 12500 is expected to be 
fully operationalised by 31st March, 2025. The Commi ttee, accordingly, recommends the 
Ministry to expedite the process. The Committee desires that the Ministry should encourage 
State/ UT Governments to monitor the process, so that operationalization of AAM (Ayush) may 
be achieved in time bound manner. 
(Para 3.21.4 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.20.1 As reported by States/UTs, 12500 AAMs (Ayush) is now fully operationalized. 
 
1.21 The Committee has taken note of the key concerns that the Ministry has submitted before the 
Committee and recommends change in institutiona l mechanism by incorporating a position of 
Mission Director with relevant seniority and experience to lead NAM implementation in each 
State/UT with exclusive assignment of NAM activities in state(s) concerned. The Committee 
further suggests appointment of dedicated District Ayush Officers responsible solely for NAM 
activities and conduct of regular orientation and capacity -building programs to ensure that the 
District Ayush Officers understand NAM objectives and implementation guidelines. The NAM is 
in last year of 5 year plan, the Ministry should take proactive steps for extending the Mission in 
ensuing financial years and secure appropriate funds for the effective implementation of the 
Mission objective. 
(Para 3.21.5) 
Action Taken  
1.21.1 Under NAM, there is a provision of District Ayush Society (DAS) and District Ayush 
Officer has been designated as Chief Executive Officer in its Governing Body and Executive 
body. The States/UTs shall make necessary arrangements for forming the District Ayush Society 
and e stablishment of District Programme Management Unit (DPMU - Ayush), recruitment and 
posting of required staff. Being a legal entity, the DAS can set up its own office which has 
adequate staff and experts and can evolve its own rules and procedures for hiring  the staff and 
experts both from the open market as well as on deputation from the Government.  Further, time 
to time different training programmes, orientation programmes, Training of Trainers (ToT) for 
manpower like Nodal Officers, Community Health Office rs, ASHAs, Yoga Instructors etc. are 
being conducted for capacity building. Mid Term Evaluation by third party of Centrally 
Sponsored Schemes including NAM is being undertaken by NITI Aayog. On the basis of 
recommendation of Mid Term Evaluation, proposal o f continuation of NAM scheme will be 

27 
 
taken up with Department of Expenditure, Government of India as flagship scheme with adequate 
fund allocation.

1.22 The Committee has been given to understand that NER is facing hurdles in effective AYUSH 
implementation due to cultural, ethnic and linguistic diversity. The geography of the NER also 
adds to the issue. Accordingly the Committee recommends that some kind of diversity training be 
conceived for the healthcare professionals and staff alike for better communication with 
patience. Modern technology may be utilized for effective communication through video 
conferencing, collaborative tools, and project management platforms. 
(Para 3.22.4 ibid)

communication gap and promote trust and cooperation.

medicine along with Yoga sessions to bridge the cultural gap and popularize the AYUSH system 
of medicines 
It has established a peripheral OPD at Smit Village to enhance the availability of A YUSH

1.23 The Committee observes with concern that the North East Region (NER) faces several 
challenges in the AYUSH sector, including the non -availability of AYUSH medicines, a limited 
number of AYUSH colleges (one in Assam, two in Meghalaya, and one in Arunachal Pradesh), 
and a shortage of specialized doctors. The Committee wants to highlight that NER despite the 
home ground of numerous herbals and rare Medicinal Plants, there is a lack of awareness about 
the AYUSH system of medicine, further hindering its acceptance and accessibility. The region 
also suffers from a shortage of technical human resources, impacting the effective 
implementation and expansion of AYUSH healthcare services. The Committee therefore, strongly 
recommends the Ministry to work upon all these issues. 
(Para 3.22.5 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.23.1 P&E Division 
NEIAH 
NEIAH has conducted community outreach programs, health camps, an d AYUSH awareness 
drives in collaboration with local bodies, educational institutions, and NGOs in order to address 
the lack of awareness about the AYUSH system. IEC (Information, Education & 
Communication) materials in local languages are being developed and distributed. 
The Institute has opened a Peripheral OPD in Ayurveda and Homoeopathy OPD at Smit areas, 
East Khasi Hills district of Meghalaya to promote Ayurveda and Homoeopathy. 
The Institute also conducted  Health camps under the Pradhan MantriJanjati  Adivasi Nyaya 
MahaAbhiyan (PM-JANMAN) programmes.  
The Institute regularly gives free consultation in the Institute Hospitals (both OPD & IPD) and

POSHAN ABHIYAN 
1.25 The Committee is of considered view that PoshanAbhiyaan can be further strengthened 
through AYUSH research activities aimed at identifying challenges and gaps in the 
implementation of nutrition services in rural, semi -urban, and tribal areas. The Committee also 
considers that ayurvedic interventions in curbing specific issues like malnutrition, sickle cell 
anemia etc., should be implemented to address a wider class of beneficiaries es pecially in rural 
and tribal area. The results of such nutritional intervention may be monitored over 2 -3 years, to 
generate a specific database of efficacy of such interventions. Moreover, POSHAN ABHIYAN 
through AYUSH interventions will certainly provide an alternative approach to tackle issues 
affecting children and adolescents on a larger scale, the Committee feels so. 
(Para 4.5.2 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.25.1 Scheme Section 
Noted.

 CCRAS 
 CCRAS implemented POSHAN ABBHIYAN through its 31 peri pheral institutes since 
inception of the program.  
 In POSHAN ABBHIYAN, Institutes of CCRAS distribute Nutritional Kits, Nutritional 
Supplements, Fruits and development of Kitchen gardens/Nutri gardens. 

33

STATUS OF AYUSH EDUCATION 
1.26 The Mini stry also proposed before the Committee that pay parity be maintained between 
allopathy doctors and Ayush practitioners. The Committee is of considered view that the AYUSH 
system of medicine is still in nascent stage. Despite all the work being done in las t decade the 
system is still not a household name as it is for the conventional system of medicine. Thus 
Committee is of view that the matter may be considered by the Govt. Of India once the AYUSH 
system has come at a appreciable footing in comparison to a llopathy. The Committee, however, 
recommends putting in a thought -through incentivisation schemes at different verticals to 
encourage the manpower to put in more effort to excel in this field, therein ensuring accelerated 
growth of AYUSH system. Further so me form of mechanism like incentivisation, mandatory 
service etc to ensure that doctors consider serving in rural areas villages etc as an opportunity to 
explore fresh vistas for Ayush Sector. 
 (Para 4.6.5 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.26.1 AEP – III Section 
● NCH 
The provision has been made in the regulation for the pay - parity with other conventional system 
of medicine. 
Scheme Section 
Noted.

serving the general public in the regular manners that helps the beneficiaries of its medical 
services and health -care services in much satisfaction.  The services being provided by the 
Institute and its staff is received in good sense and appreciation by the beneficiaries.

● NEIAH

promotions to all Ayush Practitioners. 
● NISR

visibility of the Siddha system across the country. 
● NIUM

implemented. Rural areas will benefit better by employing persons for the local population as 
DavaSaaz, Medical Officers, etc. 
NAM Section 
Under Ayushman Arogya Mandir (Ayush), there is provision of Team based incentives for HWC 
team, ASHA incentives and Performance linked Incentives for regular Medical Officer posted at 
Ayush D ispensaries acting as CHO. 12500 Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (Ayush) have been 
approved by upgrading Ayush dispensaries and Sub Health Centres for operationalization in the 
States/UTs. As reported by State/UT Governments, all 12500 AAM (Ayush) are functional. 
These AAM (Ayush) are mostly situated in rural areas. 
 
ONE NATION ONE HERB ONE STANDARD 
1.27 The Committee has taken into account the progress in the One Herb One Standard initiative 
and recommends the Ministry to further take the initiative at accelerated  rate to publish the 
standards under One Herbs One Standard by adopting and upgrading the available standard for 
Indian Medicine and Homoeopathy. The Committee is of the considered view that MoU signed 
and exchanged between IPC and the PCIM&H would certain ly provide and facilitate the one 
herb and one standard by setting unified standard for herbal medicine which would improve drug 
quality and safety across the country. 
(Para 4.7.1 ibid) 

40 
 
Action Taken  
1.27.1 PCIM&H, in collaboration with Indian Pharmacopoei a Commission (IPC) is continuously 
developing harmonized monographs under One Herb One Standard initiative. So far, 50 
monographs have been developed, and are in the process of publication.   
 
Medical Value Travel (MVT) 
1.28 The Committee notes that as a p art of Heal-in-India Campaign, the Government, keeping in 
view the fact that the cost of medical treatment in India is very low as compared to many 
developed countries, introduced of a special Visa category, Ayush Visa in 2023. The 
announcement was made at  the Global Ayush Investment and Innovation Summit (GAIIS) in 
Gandhinagar, Gujarat back in April 2022 to facilitate foreign nationals travels to India seeking 
Ayush Therapy. The Ministry of Ayush has also signed an MoU with the Tourism Ministry in this 
regard. Considering that the Ayush based healthcare and wellness economy is growing fast, the 
Committee recommends that the Ministry of Ayush may develop state of the art Ayush healthcare 
and wellbeing centers at strategic tourist places in India. The Committ ee is of the view that MVT 
and Ayush Visa scheme is a great opportunity for growth of Ayush healthcare globally. The 
Committee further recommends that to make India as a medical tourism destination of the world, 
the Ministry may develop a one stop portal f or Heal in India and explore collaboration with 
private sector in healthcare and tourism industry in this regard. The Committee would also like 
to be appraised of the details of healthcare availed and the feedback of travelers in respect of 
1646 Ayush Visas issued between 1.1.2024 to 4.2.2025. 
(Para 4.10 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.28.1  ADS Section 
The Ministry of Ayush organized the first Ayush -based Medical Value Travel (MVT) Summit 
2024 on 30.09.2024 in Mumbai for the Western region, themed ―Global Synergy in  Ayush: 
Transforming Health and Wellness through Medical Value Travel.‖ Subsequently, the second 
Ayush-based MVT Summit 2024 was held on 26.05.2025 in Chennai for the Southern region, 
with the theme ―Ayush Medical Value Travel – Promoting Wellness, Strengthening Global Ties.‖ 
These summits were aimed at strengthening India‘s position in the global Medical Value Travel 
(MVT) sector by promoting the integration of traditional Indian systems of medicine with 
modern healthcare frameworks. The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) launched 
India‘s official portal for Medical Value Travel (MVT), titled the Advantage Healthcare India 
Portal, on 17th August 2023 during the G -20 Health Ministers‘ Meeting held in Gandhinagar, 
under India‘s G -20 Presidency. Thi s "one -stop" digital platform has been developed and is 
maintained by MoHFW with the objective of simplifying and enhancing the experience of 

41 
 
international medical travellers by providing credible, comprehensive, and streamlined 
information related to healthcare services in India. 
A total of 1,507 Ayush Visas (excluding Ayush Attendant Visas) have been availed by foreign 
nationals from various countries. Of these, 149 were issued as Regular Ayush Visas ( Annexure- 
I), while 1,358 were availed through the e -Ayush Visa facility ( Annexure- II). The details of e -
Ayush Visas, including treatment -wise, country -wise ( Annexure- III), and state -wise 
(Annexure- IV) distribution, are enclosed for reference.  Further, Ministry of Home Affairs has 
intimated that they do no t have any existing mechanism for collection of the sought information 
feedback from the travelers we have awaited Ayush Visa services in India. Hence NIL inputs 
may be treated on part of MHA in this context. 
 
1.29 The Committee has come across complaints regarding unregistered persons doing illegal 
medical practice in the name of Ayush and Ayush Doctors prescribing scheduled allopathic 
drugs maligning the reputation of Ayush healthcare in the country. The Committee believes there 
are provisions under the N ational Commission for Indian System of Medicine Act, the National 
Commission for Homeopathy Act and the Drugs and Cosmetics Act to check such malpractices. 
(Para 4.11 ibid) 
Action Taken  
1.29.1 As per Section 34 of NCISM Act, 2020, NCH Act, 2020 and NMC A ct, 2019, only 
practitioners registered in the respective medical systems are legally permitted to practice that 
system. These Acts clearly prohibit cross -practice and authorize the respective commissions to 
take action against unregistered or unauthorized  practitioners. Accordingly, NCISM and NCH 
can act against unregistered individuals practicing in the name of Ayush, similarly in cases where 
persons prescribing scheduled allopathic drugs without having the required recognized 
qualification fall under the jurisdiction of the NMC and NMC can take action as per section 34 of 
NMC Act, 2019. 
Ayush Vision @2047 
1.30 The Committee takes note of various components of Ayush Vision@2047 that interalia cover 
Strategic Areas (Research, Education & Capacity Building, Healthcare, Manufacturing& 
Services) opportunities (Governance & Innovation Governance, Global Value Chain) that would 
pave the way of maximum promotion and popularization of Ayush Sector in domestic market and 
global fora. The Committee is of the consider ed view that application of Ayush in Clinical 
healthcare, biochemistry, lifestyle illness would lead to standardization of Ayush research & 
medicine system and thus contributing to evidence based solution, thereby boosting the new 
paradigm shift in Ayush s phere. The Committee believes that identification of strategic course of 
action education & capacity building healthcare and manufacturing & services would go a long 
in furtherance of Ayush Sector. The Committee strongly visualize that a lot of opportuniti es are

awaiting in areas of governance of tariff lines, formulation and standardization & quality of 
Ayush Sector. Needless to say, quality certification and labs & healthcare NABL Accreditation of 
all labs would stamp Ayush Sector is the global market. 
(Para 5.1.2 ibid)

CHAPTER–III 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS IN RESPECT OF WHICH REPLIES OF 
THE MINISTRY HAVE NOT BEEN ACCEPTED BY THE COMMITTEE  
 
Staff strength of the Ministry 
3.1 The Committee observes that against the sanctioned strength of 78 posts in various levels a 
total of 53 officers are in -position thereby there is a vacan cy in 25 posts, especially at middle 
and lower level, which must be hampering the operational efficiency of the Ministry. The 
Committee, therefore, desires that recruitment process should be expedited to fill up the vacant 
posts at the earliest for efficient working of the Ministry. 
 (Para 1.4.1 ibid) 
Action Taken 
3.1.1 Establishment – II Section 
It is informed that Officers/Officials from the Level of Assistant Section Officer to Secretary in 
the Ministry are posted by DoPT. The recruitment process in this  regard is carried out by 
SSC/UPSC etc. At present, the sanctioned strength of CSCS cadre in the Ministry  is to be 
clarified by DoPT for further course of action. The Ministry is actively taking up the matter with 
DoPT for filling up of the vacant positions in the Ministry. 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.1.2 The Committee notes that the appointment of officers/officials from the Level of 
Assistant Section Officer to Secretary in the Ministry is being done by DoPT and the 
recruitment process is carried out by SSC/UPSC. Given that the sanctioned strength staff is 
short of a vacancy of 25 posts which must have been affecting the operati onal efficiency of 
the Ministry, the Committee, therefore, reiterates that the Ministry of Ayush must take up 
the matter with DoPT to fill up the vacant posts, the sooner the better. 
 
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION  
3.2 The Committee takes note of the variations between projected demand and allocated BE and 
the budget utilization trend of the Ministry of Ayush. Given the Ministry‟s larger vision  to 
position Ayush systems as a preferred system of attaining a healthy India the Committee expects 
the Ministry to fully utilize the RE in actual expenditure and make necessary plans/policies that 
should justify for getting a higher share of the Projected  Demand in the forth -coming financial 
years. 
(Para 2.1.2 ibid) 

47

NAM Section 
Under NAM, Ministry is approving the State Annual Action Plans (SAA Ps) as received from 
States/UTs for implementation of different activities/programmes within the provision of NAM 
guidelines and also monitoring the progress of the activities as per existing provisions of 
output/outcome indicators of NAM guideline by obta ining physical & financial progress as well 
as different types of reports time to time. In addition to it, time to time different regional review 
meetings and field visits are also being conducted to monitor the progress of implementation of 
activities at the ground level.  With regard to effective implementation of NAM Scheme, the 
State/UT Governments have been requested time to time for strengthening of administrative 
setup with appropriate manpower at the level of State headquarter as well as District lev el. It is 
also pertinent to mention here that there is a dedicated provision under NAM Guideline for 
support of SPMU/DPMU to assist States/UT Governments for smooth & effective 
implementation of NAM Scheme and they have been supported for the same as per t he proposals 
received in SAAPs.  Ministry of Ayush has recently issued Indian Public Health Standard (IPHS) 
guidelines for Ayush hospitals and Ayushman Arogya Mandir (Ayush) to all States/UTs and 
requested for compliance of the same to maintain the standar d of Infrastructure &Human 
Resource in the Ayush health facilities for its better functioning and quality services.  As far as 
Ministry is concerned, a Central Programme Management Unit is also functioning having 
qualified and skilled manpower of different nature like Programme Manager, Finance Manager, 

50 
 
Account Manager, HMIS Manager, data assistant, Domain Experts etc. for doing the needful 
coordination with officials of the States/UTs including SPMU/DPMU for effective 
implementation of the Scheme. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.3.2 The Committee observes that many of the Action Taken Replies largely reflects the  
essence of   the Committee‟s original recommendations without providing concrete 
implementation strategies, specific timelines, or achievement of measura ble targets. While 
the recognition of issues such as staffing shortages and budgetary adjustments is just noted 
by the Ministry with out entailing concrete action taken or the absence of detailed action 
plans which severely undermines effective monitoring. The Committee, therefore, reiterates 
that the Ministry must ensure submission of comprehensive implementation frameworks, 
clearly outlining timelines, recruitment strategies, and monitoring mechanisms aligned with 
the Annual Action Plan and Output/Outcome Framework. Future submissions should 
reflect substantive progress and accountability rather than general assurances or 
reiteration of the recommendations made by the Committee. The Ministry has just 
mentioned that time to time different regional review mee tings and field visits are being 
conducted to monitor the progress of implementation of activities under NAM at the 
ground level, however no substantive fact is furnished in compliance with the Committee‟s 
recommendations. While the Committee reposes its f aith in the Ministry, therefore, the 
Committee expects the Ministry to furnish the details of actual development activities as 
envisaged under NAM with absolute transparency. The Committee, herefore recommends 
that the Ministry provide comprehensive measur able implementation statistics going 
forward. 
 
3.4 The Committee observes that the projected demand of the Ministry for the year 2025 -26 is 
Rs. 5408.48 crore against which an amount of Rs. 3992.90 crore has been allocated in BE 2025 -
26 thus there is a shor tfall of Rs. 1415.58 crore. The Committee expresses its concern that 
reduced budgetary allocation to the Ministry of Ayush in comparison to the projected demand 
would have an adverse impact on the implementation of originally conceived 
projects/schemes/programmes. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Ministry of Ayush 
to approach the Ministry of Finance to seek higher budgetary allocation at the supplementary 
demand stage to ensure that no undertaken project/scheme gets suffered for want of requisite 
fund.                    (Para 2.3.1 ibid) 
 
Action Taken  
3.4.1 Budget Section  
The recommendation of the Committee has been noted for compliance. 
 

51 
 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.4.2 The Committee notes that the Ministry was able to utilize 99.17% of the BE for t he 
year 2024-25, however, the Committee observes that there has been significant gap/shortfall 
of Rs. 1414.16 crores in BE 2025 -26 between Projected Demand and Allocated fund to the 
Ministry. The Committee expresses concern that the Ministry of Finance is either not 
allocating adequate budget to the Ministry of Ayush or the proposals placed  by the 
Ministry of Ayush before the Ministry of Finance for higher allocation is not sufficiently 
convincing. Further, such variations recurring oftentimes affect the ac hievement of set 
targets in cherished schemes of the Ministry  of Ayush . The Committee, therefore, desires 
that the Ministry of Finance should take cognizance of such financial gap between 
Projected Demand and Allocated fund, and look into the matter with d ue consideration at 
RE stage. The Committee reiterates that reduced budgetary allocation to the Ministry of 
Ayush, in comparison to the projected demand, would have an adverse impact on the 
implementation of originally conceived projects/schemes/programmes . The Committee, 
therefore, again recommends the Ministry of Ayush to approach the Ministry of Finance to 
seek higher budgetary allocation at the supplementary demand stage to ensure that no 
undertaken project/scheme gets suffered for want of requisite fund.  
 
3.5 The Committee in its 108th Report and 128th Report has recommended allocation of 5% and 
10% of total health budget, yet the budget allocation is been slowly reduced from year 2023 -24, 
2024-25 and 2025 -26 in comparison to that of Dept. of Health an d Family Welfare. The 
Committee recommends the Ministry to place strong proposals before the Ministry of Finance 
backed by visionary schemes/programmes that would warrant a higher budgetary allocation. 
The Committee expects the Ministry to reach the 10% mark in the ensuing financial years.  
                   (Para 2.4.1 ibid)

The recommendation of the Committee has been noted for compliance. 
 
Further Recommendation

recommendation for allocation of atleast 10% of total health budget to the Ministry of 
Ayush, however, the actual budget allocation to the Ministry has not been increased 
adequately. The Ministry has just furnished, in its Action  Taken Note, that 
recommendation of the Committee has been noted for compliance  which reflects the 
reluctant approach of the Ministry of Ayush . The Committee expects that the Ministry 
would furnish the response of the Ministry of Finance to the proposal of  the Ministry of

OUTPUT-OUTCOME MONITORING FRAMEWORK (2025-26) 
 
3.6 The Committee takes notes of the various details of Output -Outcome Framework of the 
schemes of Ministry of Ayush to be executed during 2025 -26. The Committee recommends the 
Ministry of Ayush to have resilient and alert mode of monitoring mechanism to oversee the 
execution of the Schemes/Projects to ensure that the physical targets as set in the Output -
Outcome Framework is achieved without time and cost overruns. The Committee, in this regard, 
recommends the Ministry to undertake midterm performance evaluation of each scheme and 
projects through Cost Benefit Analysis, targets set vs targets achieved. The ministry may publish 
such analysis in public d omain to increase the faith of public in the Ministry‟s on ground 
performance. The Committee strongly believes that the Output -Outcome Framework will enrich 
the enabling environment of the Ministry of Ayush and bring forth the catalytic changes in 
providing Ayurvedic and Homeopathic mode of treatment to all. 
(Para 2.6.1 ibid) 
 
 
Action Taken 
3.6.1 Ministry  of Ayush is actively monitoring the physical progress of the CS/CSS Schemes of  
Ministry of Ayush and updating the same on the OOMF dashboard  on quarterly basis. Further, 
DMEO NITI Aayog also  reviews the  OOMF progress  and expenditure 
annually Ministry of Ayush as also  initiated the third party Evaluation process  of 
Central Sector Schemes of the Ministry. In addition, there are various Committees of the Centr al 
sector schemes such as Project Approval Committee who regularly monitors the functioning and 
performance of the scheme. 
 
Further Recommendation  
3.6.2 The Committee notes the Ministry‟s response regarding ongoing monitoring 
mechanisms and third -party ev aluations mechanism to oversee the execution of the 
Schemes/Projects to ensure that the physical targets as set in the Output -Outcome 
Framework is achieved without time and cost overruns . However, the Committee observes 
that the Action Taken Reply , as furnished by the Ministry of Ayush , does not sufficiently 
address the Committee‟s specific recommendation in its letter and spirit for want of  
assurance of conducting structured midterm performance evaluations , incorporating cost-
benefit analysis and a compar ative assessment of targets achieved against targets set. 
Additionally, no action taken has been reflected in regard to the publication of such 
evaluations in the public domain to promote transparency and public confidence. The 
Committee, therefore, reiter ates the need for comprehensive mid -term evaluations, 
inclusion of financial efficiency metrics, and proactive disclosure of outcomes to ensure 
timely course correction and enhanced accountability to bring forth the catalytic changes in 
providing Ayurvedic and Homeopathic mode of treatment to all. 
 

53 
 
3.7 The Committee notes the Ministry aims to enhance AYUSH services, particularly in 
underserved and remote areas, but lacks the resources to do so effectively. The need to establish 
integrated AYUSH hospitals in  many districts has a high financial requirement. The Committee 
also observes that the Ministry is not fully utilizing the funds allocated to it. It's crucial to 
optimize resource utilization by implementing strategies that improve the efficiency and 
effectiveness of existing programs, focusing on high -impact interventions in priority areas, and 
establishing robust tracking systems to monitor spending and identify the most effective resource 
allocation. Simultaneously, a strategic approach to infrastructure  expansion is necessary, 
prioritizing the establishment of integrated AYUSH hospitals in underserved districts through a 
phased approach that aligns with available resources, while also exploring cost -effective models 
for hospital establishment and operation. 
(Para 2.7.1 ibid) 
Action Taken 
3.7.1 NAM Section 
Public Health being a State Subject, the primary responsibility to bridge infrastructure gaps with 
respect to Ayush system in the country including rural and underserved regions  lies with the 
respective State/UT Governments. However, under National Ayush Mission (NAM) there is 
provision for establishment of integrated Ayush hospitals and State/UT Governments may project 
their demand as per the felt need through State Annual Action Plans (SAAPs) to cover r ural and 
underserved regions of the country. 
Further, the monthly/quarterly reports on various parameters are also being obtained from the 
States/UTs to access the physical/ financial progress of different approved activities of NAM. 
Regional Review Meetin gs, Field visits and One to one meetings have also been conducted to 
analyze the progress of NAM scheme and to facilitate State/UT for effective implementation of 
the activities in the field.  
 
Further Recommendation  
 
3.7.2 The Committee has been apprised  by the Ministry that Public Health, being a State 
Subject, the primary responsibility to bridge infrastructure gaps with respect to Ayush 
system in the country, including rural and underserved regions,  lies with the respective 
State/UT Governments.  The Committee finds the response of the Ministry evasive  which 
appears that the Ministry is shrugging of its responsibi lity. The Committee understands 
that the Ministry has a goal to enhance AYUSH services, particularly, in underserved and 
remote areas, but Sta tes/UTs lacks the requisite resources to do so, effectively. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry should undertake steps for 
encouraging States/UTs to propose under State Annual Action Plans (SAAP) for 
establishment of integrated Ayush Ho spitals in backward regions of the Country 
specifically districts with low human development index in the State of Orissa, Jharkhand, 
Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan and Bihar (particularly in Koshi/Simanchal Region covering 
Purnia, Kathiar, Saharsa, Madipura, F orbesganj, Araria, Kishanganj). The Committee, 

54 
 
therefore, desires that the Ministry take a systematic, analytical approach in assessing 
parameters and identification of sites in underserved districts for establishing of Ayush 
Medical College and Hospital i n a time bound manner to effectively implement its 
cherished goals of placing AYUSH system of healthcare in the remotest parts of the 
country, even if it needs handholding the states in creating SAAPs in regard to AYUSH. 
 
3.8 The National Ayush Mission (NA M), being a centrally sponsored scheme where Center and 
States have fund share like 60% and 40%. The Committee was apprised that some States/ UTs 
express their inability to contribute their share. Due to the resource crunch, the Mission is not 
effective in  achieving goals. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Ministry to provide 
financial assistance schemes for on -boarding the States/UTs on NAM. The Ministry may also 
explore the possibility with the MoF to change the contribution ratio of the Centre and  State to 
70:30 for encouraging the states to get onboard NAM.  
(Para 2.7.2 ibid) 
Action Taken  
3.8.1 NAM Section 
As far as contribution ratio of the Center and State (70:30) is concerned, it falls under the 
purview of MoF as it is based on overall scheme structure approved for Centrally Sponsored 
Schemes across Central Government Ministries/Department. 
 
Further Recommendation  
3.8.2 The Committee understands that the contribution ratio of Centre and State in a 
Centrally Sponsored Scheme falls under the pur view of the Ministry of Finance. The 
Ministry of Ayush may take the matter of modification in existing ratio of 60% and 40% to 
70% and 30%  of Centre and State , so that economically weaker States may become part 
and parcel of the National Ayush Mission (NAM ). Since, the implementation of the NAM 
Scheme is mandated under Ministry of Ayush, it is imperative on the part of the Ministry of 
Ayush that no stone is left unturned by the Ministry in convincing a change in the 
contribution ratio. The Committee, strong ly recommends the Ministry of Ayush to intiate 
communication with Ministry of Finance for increased allocation and the change in the 
contribution ratio to achieve cherished objective of the scheme. The Committee further 
recommends the Ministry to explore t he possibility of providing financial assistance and 
other forms of incentives so as to bring onboard the States/UTs that are unable to 
contribute their share into NAM scheme. 
 
 

55 
 
MINISTRY‟S PROPOSAL TO 15TH FINANCE COMMISSION 
 
3.9 The 15th Finance Commissio n is till the 31.3.2026. The Committee observes that the M/o 
Ayush must strive hard to integrate Ayush into mainstream healthcare. Besides providing Ayush 
services through PHCs, CHCs and DHs, significant efforts are required in quality improvement, 
research and regulatory framework in Ayush healthcare, global collaboration, AYUSH market 
expansion, sustainable development of medicinal plants etc. All this would not be possible 
without adequate development of skilled Health Human Resource in the Ayush sector.  The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry should come up with mega schemes in 
mission mode. The Committee recommends that the Ministry take proactive steps for 
correspondence with 16th Finance Commission and accordingly chalk out plan for ade quate 
fund allocation and timely release of the same for the five years thence for accelerated growth of 
AYUSH. 
(Para 2.7.3 ibid) 
 
Action Taken

Strengthening Ayush for National Health. A team led by Hon‘ble Minister of State (I/C) of 
Ministry of Ayushrepresented Ministry of Ayush.  
 IC Section 
The Central Sector Scheme for Promotion of International Co -operation in Ayush aims to 
promote and strengthen awareness and interest in Ayush Systems of Medicine at the international 
level. The scheme has specific objectives to facilitate the international promotion, development, 
and recognition of Ayurveda, Yoga, Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, Sowa -Rigpa, and 
Homoeopathy. The overarching goals include fostering interaction among stakeholders, 
supporting the international exchange of experts and information, boosting global trade in Ayush 
products and services, and promoting academics and research through the establishment of Ayush 
Academic Chairs in foreign countries.

AIIA 
All India Institute of Ayurveda (AIIA) is an autonomous institution under the Ministry of Ayush, 
Govt of India and affiliated to Delhi University for academic purposes. AIIA is providing 
following services and activities to the communities of the country: 
1. Healthcare Services: AIIA serving as a tertiary care hospital (200 beds in New Delhi and 250 
beds in Goa), offering OPD and IPD services. It has provided holistic healthcare to millions, 
integrating Ayurvedic princi ples with modern diagnostics and treatment. Since inception, the 
institute has provided holistic health care to more than 27 lakh patients on OPD basis and nearly 
14,300 patients on IPD basis through 45 specialized OPDs on strength areas of Ayurveda. For 
excellence in health care services, AIIA Hospital is accredited with NABH consecutively for 3 
term. 
2. Research and Innovation: AIIA is committed to advancing Ayurvedic research and has 
collaborated with national and international institutes. It has establi shed labs for drug testing, 
pharmacology, informatics and molecular biology, facilitating cutting-edge research.  
3. Education and Training: AIIA offers postgraduate and PhD programs, skilling courses, and 
entrepreneurship initiatives, fostering interdisciplinary learning and human resource development 
in Ayurveda. Nearly 617 and 144 scholars were offered post graduate and PhD degrees 
respectively across 12 specialties in Delhi campus. AIIA Goa enrolled 300 students for under 
graduation program and recently  started PG programs in 5 subjects. Nearly 301 skilled in 
Ayurveda therapy etc have been awarded and about 300 are enrolled under DAKSHYA skill 
training programs. 
4. Public Health Initiatives: AIIA has undertaken six public health projects for benefitting the 
community at large in Delhi NCR region on Management of musculo -skeletal pain through 
Ayurvedic intervention; Promotion of physical and mental health in elderly population; Ayurveda 
care for healthy progeny; Promotion of Ayurvedic medicine &Rasayana th erapy for management 
of Latent tuberculosis etc.  
5. Global Recognition and Collaboration: AIIA has garnered international acclaim, hosting 
dignitaries like the Director -General of the World Health Organization. It has signed numerous 
82 MOUs (18 international) with premier institutions worldwide, fostering collaborative research 
and exchange programs.  
6. Infrastructure Development: AIIA is expanding its infrastructure spanning over 22 acres, with 
ongoing construction projects in three phases (first phase completed) including hospital, Covid 
Centre, residential quarters, auditoriums, sports complex, and hostels to accommodate growing 
demands and public health.  

58 
 
7. Extension Services: AIIA has extended its services through satellite centre at Goa and 
collaborations with institutions across India, ensuring broader access to holistic Ayurvedic 
healthcare at Kevadia, Gujarat, Supreme Court of India, National Cancer Institute, Indian 
Institute of Technology-Delhi and Safdarjung Hospital etc.  
8. Academic Excellen ce: AIIA's commitment to academic excellence is recognized through its 
NAAC accreditation obtaining A++ and contributing to the advancement of Ayurvedic 
knowledge in more than 1600 publication in esteemed journals. 
9. Service excellence: AIIA is accredited  with ISO 9001:2015 Certification for its service 
excellence.

3.11.1 NMPB Section 
National Medicinal Plants Board, Ministry of AYUSH also supports projects under integrated component 
namely ―Forward and backward linkage in supply chain of medicinal plants in the existing   Central Sector 
Scheme on Conservation, Development and Sustainable Management of Medici nal Plants to encourage 
the farmers by providing the facilities like nursery to raise the quality planting material/seeds, 
infrastructure for post -harvest management units (storage, drying sheds), value addition, marketing, 
sensitization  programs for skil l development, certification for quality raw material in a synergistic 
manner. Besides this, NMPB supports establishment of nurseries to raise the planting material among the 
farmers. NMPB also supports project for IEC activities to promote the medicinal plants sector. 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.11.2 The Committee appreciates the efforts of the National Medicinal Plants Board 
(NMPB) in supporting farmers and stakeholders through infrastructure, skill development, 
and quality certification. However, to fully realize the potential of the medicinal plants 
sector as a catalyst of economic growth and livelihood enhancement, the Committee 
recommends that NMPB further strengthen its initiatives by promoting entrepreneurship 
and innovation. This includes launching in cubation programs, facilitating market access 
through digital platforms, and piloting cluster -based and community -owned models. 
Special focus should be given to tribal and marginalized communities to integrate 
traditional knowledge into formal value chains . Additionally, a robust monitoring and 
evaluation framework should be established to assess the impact of these initiatives, 

63 
 
ensuring sustainable and inclusive growth of all stakeholders. The Committee firmly 
believes that effective implementation of Cent ral Sector Scheme in Conservation, 
Development and Sustainable Management of Medicinal Plants would go a long way in 
promoting medicinal plant sector. Therefore, NMPB must formulate innovative schemes so 
that farmers engaged in cultivation of medicinal pla nts would enhance their livelihood 
system.  
 
PHARMACOPOEIA COMMISSION FOR INDIAN MEDICINE AND HOMOEOPATHY 
3.12 The Committee strongly recommends the consolidation of all AYUSH drug -related 
standard-setting processes under a single authoritative body viz. a n independent drug controller 
of AYUSH, in alignment with the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, and its associated rules. To 
achieve this, the Ministry should establish a streamlined and inclusive mechanism that actively 
involves stakeholders in the developme nt of pharmacopoeial standards, ensuring greater 
efficiency and uniformity. Additionally, the Committee emphasizes that PCIM&H and CCRAS 
may come together to coordinate and collaborate in this initiative to enhance the scientific 
testing and evaluation of a larger number of ASU&H drug samples to ensure safety, efficacy, and 
quality strengthening the foundation of research and standardization in the sector. 
(Para 3.3.5 ibid) 
Action Taken  
3.12.1 L&P Section (PCIM&H) 
● For development of Pharmacopoeia and Formu laries the technical support from the 
research councils namely CCRAS, CCRUM, CCRS and CCRH has been taken.  
● A formal entering into MoU with these Research Councils namely CCRAS, CCRUM, 
CCRS, CCRH are under progress. 
 
Ayurveda Section 
● CCRAS 
● The CCRAS and PC IM&H coordinated and collaborated in developing quality standards 
for single drugs and compound formulations of ASU&H, which were published in the 
Ayurvedic Pharmacopoeia of India, Part -I and Part -II volumes and further similarly 
CCRAS sharing the data of 22 single drug monographs and 3 compound formulations for 
publication of Pharmacopoeial monographs.  
● The CCRAS and PCIM&H laboratories are accredited by NABL (ISO/IEC 17025:2017) 
and carry out inter -laboratory comparisons to ensure competence in providing testing 
services of excellence to the stakeholders of  ASU&H drugs.  
 
 
 

64 
 
Further recommendation 
 
3.12.2 The Committee is dismayed to observe that the Committee‟s recommendation for a 
single independent drug controller for Ayush hasn‟t been complied with yet . The 
Committee thus reiterates its recommendation for consolidating all AYUSH drug -related 
standard-setting processes under a single authoritative body viz. an independent drug 
controller of AYUSH, in alignment with the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940, and its 
associated rules. To achieve this, the Ministry should establish a streamlined and inclusive 
mechanism that actively involves stakeholders in the  development of pharmacopoeial 
standards uniformly, ensuring enhanced safety, quality and efficacy of Ayush  medicines 
nationwide. For that sake and also for strengthening the foundation of research and 
uniform standardization in Ayush Sector, PCIM&H & various research councils of Ayush 
viz. CCRAS, CCRUM, CCRS and CCRH work together with due co -ordination and 
collaboration.
