AYUSH OUSHADI GUNVATTA EVAM UTTAPADAN SAMVARDHAN YOJANA 
(AOGUSY) 
 
3.15The Committee is of considered view that a separate body be created to  implement and 
oversee the Pharmacovigilance of ASU&H drugs including surveillance of misleading 
advertisements etc. countrywide. The Committee feels the need for strengthening of Central and 
State regulatory frameworks including Technical Human Resource &  Capacity Building 
programs for Ayush drugs for Manufacturing of quality medicine from state and private 
pharmacies and NABL accreditation of DTL. The Committee observes that Ministry should make 
concerted efforts for improved surveillance of adverse drug reactions through NPvCC, IPvCs 
and PPvCs. The Committee understands that in order to get the support for development of 
standards and accreditation/ certification of Ayush products & materials, the collaboration with 
BIS, QCI and other relevant scientific institutions and industrial R&D centres is all the more 

66 
 
necessary to achieve the cherished objectives of the Yojana. The Committee desires that the 
Ministry should strive in this direction. 
 
(Para 3.9.8 ibid) 
 
Action Taken  
 
3.15.1  L&P Section (PCIM&H) 
● The commission develops and amend the Pharmacopoeias and Formularies of Ayurveda, 
Siddha, Unani and Homoeopathy System of Medicines at regular intervals to ascertain the 
identity, purity and strength of ASU&H medicines.  
● Act as a Central Drug cum Testing Laboratory 
PCIM&H also act as an appellate drug testing laboratory receives the samples from Govt. 
agencies as per Drugs & Cosmetics Act & Rules there under for ascertaining their quality. 
● Capacity Building Training Programs:  PCIM&H regularly conducts capacit y-building 
training sessions for stakeholders, including drug regulators, enforcement authorities, and 
technical staff from ASU&H drug testing laboratories. 
● The labs of PCIM&H are NABL accreditated for chemical and biological testing 
● PCIM&H is also supporting the various Ayush sectional committees of BIS  
Drug Policy Section 
Considering the current growing demands and concerns towards traditional systems of medicine 
(Ayush in Indian context), across the globe; creation of a separate platform to exclusively 
implement, facilitate, and monitor the Pharmacovigilance is highly essential. A functional 
working module may be planned in such a way to include all states and union territories of the 
country and manned with qualified, and trained exclusive manpower with  specific objectives to 
achieve in a time bound manner. 
Taking the experience of previous pharmacovigilance programs for Ayush drugs (during 2007 
and 2017), a robust system with determined objective of generating skilled human resources and 
capacity buildi ng of various stakeholders in association with the Indian Pharmacopoeia 
Commission (a WHO Collaborating Center for Pharmacovigilance). 
Besides the objectives of having surveillance of suspected ADRs, and objectionable 
advertisements; an additional componen t of financial support may be earmarked for possible 
research component exclusively to evaluate herb -drug interactions and further to evaluate safety 
aspect of Ayush drugs. 
 

67 
 
Further recommendation 
 
3.15.2 The Committee appreciates the role of a Pharmacopoe ia Commission in developing 
and amending Pharmacopoeia and formularies for Ayush system and thus setting 
standards for Ayush drugs ensuring their quality, safety and efficacy and surveillance of 
misleading advertisement. However, the Committee reiterates t he creation of a separate 
platform/body to exclusively implement, facilitate and monitor the Pharmcoviligilance of 
Ayush Drugs. The Committee feels that surveillance of ADRs is crucial for ensuring the 
safety of drugs including those from traditional syste ms like ASU&H, IPvCs and PPvCs 
which may contribute to these efforts. The Committee recommends the Ministry to 
strengthen Pharmacovigilance systems and promote ADR reporting for protecting the 
public health through NPC -ISM, IPvCs and PPvCs which plays a vi tal role in ensuring the 
safe use of ASU&H drugs. Adequate financial support may be earmarked for research 
components exclusively to evaluate herb drug interaction and further to assess the quality 
and safety aspects of AYUSH drugs.   
 
3.16 The Committee s trongly recommends that the Ministry must chalk out strategic action plan 
to control misleading/ objectionable advertisements that have been arisen to 15,873 incidents 
during 2023-24 and 2024-25(upto December 2024). If required penal provisions may be made  to 
check/combat misleading advertisements that claim to cure lethal diseases within a short span of 
time. 
(Para 3.9.9 ibid) 
Action Taken  
3.16.1 Drug Policy Section 
The Ministry is in the process of creating Online portal for reporting of misleading/ obje ctionable 
advertisements and also track the action taken by various authorities on such reports through the 
portal. 
Further Recommendation 
3.16.2 The Committee acknowledges the Ministry‟s initiative to create an online portal for 
reporting and tracking mis leading or objectionable AYUSH advertisements. However, the 
Committee emphasizes that an online reporting mechanism alone is insufficient to address 
the growing number of violations, which reached 15,873 incidents in 2023 -24 and 2024 -25 
(up to December 202 4). The Ministry must clearly identify and empower the authorities 
responsible for investigating and taking timely action against offenders. Moreover, the 
Committee is of the considered view that strict penal provisions should be incorporated into 
the relevant laws or regulations to deter and penalize those making false and misleading  
advertisement/claims, particularly, those asserting quick cures for serious and life -
threatening diseases. The Committee further recommends that the Ministry establish a 

68 
 
transparent monitoring and reporting framework, through which it regularly updates on 
enforcement actions taken, including the number of investigations, prosecutions, penalties 
imposed, and cases resolved. This will ensure accountability, strengthen deterrence,  and 
enhance public trust in AYUSH healthcare system.

3.24.1  P&E Division 
● NAM 
North East States have been requested time to time for establishment of Project Managemen t 
Unit (PMU), so that their active support may be obtained for effective implementation of 
Scheme. Accordingly, Manipur, Assam, Nagaland, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram have 
established PMU as per provision of NAM guidelines. However, Arunachal Pradesh and  Sikkim 
have established PMU but not having full strength of manpower. 
Further Recommendation 
3.24.2 The Committee is of the considered view that the effective implementation of NAM 
scheme in NER is concomitant to establishement of Project Management Unit (PMU) with 
sufficient manpower and dedicated Ayush Directorate to ensure adequate monitoring and 
positioning of Ayush related issues. The Committee notes that although Manipur, Assam, 
Nagaland, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram have established PMU as per provis ion of NAM 
guidelines. While Arunachal Pradesh and Sikkim have although established PMU but 
without having full strength of manpower. This is really a sorry state of affairs for effective 
implementation of NAM Scheme in these two States. The Committee, the refore, strongly 
recommends the Ministry of Ayush to effectively persuade North -Eastern States for 
dedicated Ayush Directorate and PMU in Arunachal Pradesh and Sikkim must be 
equipped with adequate manpower. 
 
 
 

77 
 
3.25 The Committee takes note of the infrastr uctural projects being carried out in NER for 
AYUSH hospitals supported under NAM in NER and expects the same to be completed and 
functional within the estimated time line. The Ministry may look into projects that have been 
approved before three years or earlier and yet not functional. 
(Para 3.22.8) 
 
Action Taken  
3.25.1 P&E Division 
● NAM 
Public Health being a State Subject, the implementation of NAM Scheme comes under purview 
of respective State Government. As the proposals received from NER through SAAP, Ministry of 
Ayush has approved 16 integrated Ayush hospitals from 2014-15 to 2021-22.  
          The progress of construction of hospitals is being carried out in a phased manner, however 
there is slow progress in completing construction work in some cases are due to following facts:- 
a) In few cases construction work held up because of land disputes/ court cases.  
b) Construction work in hilly States cannot be carried out in throughout the year because of 
seasonal effects. 
c) Due to law and order conditions. 
Further, to complete the construction work of hospital, the Ministry is in regular touch with NER 
States and the central field visit teams are being sent to monitor the same. The status of progress 
of NAM activities are being regularly followed at the hi ghest level in different progress review 
meetings with States/UTs including NER. 
Resultantly, 10 integrated Ayush hospitals are functional, 03 hospitals construction completed 
and 03 under construction as reported by NER State Governments.  
Further Recommendation  
3.25.2 The Committee expresses its concern over the delay in completion of infrastructure 
projects viz. 16 integrated Ayush Hospitals approved during 2014 -15 to 2021 -22 under 
NAM Scheme through SAAP due to slow progress in completing construction work. 
However, the Committee notes that 10 integrated Ayush Hospitals are functional, 03 
Hospitals construction completed but not functional and remaining 03 are still under 
construction. The Committee, therefore, desires that three hospitals construction that have 
been completed but not functional must be made functional without further delay and 
accelerated progress must be made for completing the remaining three infrastructure 
projects so that the same is also made functional without much procrastination.  
 

78 
 
Issues Raised By Members 
3.26 During the course of the examination of Demand for Grants of the M/o Ayush on 17th 
February, 2025, the Members of the Committee raised the following issues, seeking the 
intervention of the M/o Ayush for strengthening, awar eness, promotion of Ayush Sector in the 
country and consolidation, propagation & expansion and globalization of Ayush mode of 
treatment in the foreign countries that have got expression in various parts of the Report:- 
i. Documentation of Ayush knowledge and outcome of its research & dissemination, thereof, 
in the remote and far flung regions of the country and foreign countries;  
ii. Public awareness campaigns about the benefits of Ayurveda but bulwark against 
quackery, claiming cancer treatment and cure chronic kidney disease patients on dialysis 
without kidney transplantation within a week and regulation over misleading 
advertisement claiming quick treatment of critical illnesses under Ayurveda that has 
erased the overall credibility of Ayurveda to large extent.  The rise in the deceptive 
promotion highlights enforcement gaps and inefficiencies in tackling misleading claims in 
the Ayush Sector as a whole. There should be penal provision for misleading 
advertisement;  
iii. India being Vishwa -Guru and celebrating World Y oga Day should expand Yoga 
Awareness at the Rural and Block Level throughout the year instead of only on World 
Yoga Diwas;  
iv. Avoiding competition between Allopathy and Ayurveda and adoption of cooperative & 
collaborative approach between the two systems of healthcare and Ayush system of 
healthcare should be emphasized in preventive and palliative healthcare;  
v. Joint meetings of NMC, NCISM, NCH, CCRYN etc. to resolve difference of opinion 
between modern medicine and traditional medicine and promote harmony bet ween the 
two branches of healthcare;  
vi. Keeping in view the accelerated growth rate of medical tourism vis -à-vis general tourism, 
opportunity to promote medical tourism in the region with higher medicinal plants viz. 
Kerala, Karnataka and NER;  
vii. Greater impetus to Government‟s Vision to „Go to the Villages and Rural India‟ and for 
setting up of Smart Villages, there is need to promote the momentum of medical tourism 
towards Rural India where organic food, cow dung etc. are available in plenty, thereby 
promoting PanchgavyaChikitsa (Cowpathy) which is a traditional system of Ayurvedic 
medicine that utilizes five products derived from cows for therapeutic purposes. 
viii. All out efforts be made to eliminate sickle cell anemia in tribal region and strategy to 
overcome malnutrition through medicinal plants and research thereon;  

79 
 
ix. Adequate fund allocation under the Special Component Plan for Scheduled Castes and 
Tribal Areas sub-plan for Siddha System;  
x. In the Medical Education Training and Research, adequate budgetary alloc ation be made 
to Siddha System vis-à-vis other components of Ayush;  
xi. Impetus to training and skill development of Ayush Doctors and Staff;  
xii. Establishment of Ayush Hospitals in each district and tier -2 cities may be implemented at 
an accelerated pace;  
xiii. Formulation of strategy and provision of adequate budget for setting up of Ayush 
Institutes/Yoga Ashrams in under -developed States like Bihar, Jharkhand, UttarPradesh 
and likewise;  
xiv. Facilities that can be provided to investor who wants to open Yoga Institute o r Ayurveda 
companies;  
xv. In order to promote Ayush, especially Yoga and Naturopathy, the M/o Ayush expedite the 
legislation on Yoga and Naturopathy;  
xvi. Expediting establishment of Central Research Institutes for Homeopathy in Bhubaneswar;  
xvii. Compliance of Standa rd Operating Procedures (SOPs) & procedural 
formalities/guidelines as issued by M/o Finance for seeking higher budgetary allocation 
for Ayush;  
xviii. Keeping in view the number of 7,36,000 Ayush practitioners in 2014 and 7,39,000 after 
10 years in 2024 needs to be improved by taking concrete steps;  
xix. Chalking out strategy to seek higher allocation to Ayush during the period of 16th 
Finance Commission;  
xx. Accelerated implementation of Programme on upgradation of Ayush Health Centres and 
Integrated Ayush Hospitals and  setting up of Ayush Hospital in Maharashtra and Punjab 
and other places where there is no Ayush Hospitals in order to remove inequalities in 
geographical distribution;  
xxi. Adequate fund for clinical trials and scientific validation centres of Ayush Products to 
propagate their credibility and impetus to enhance the testing capacity of these 
institutions for scientific validation and evident based research;  
xxii. Extensive research in Ayush Sector for „going back to the nature‟ correlation and 
avoidance of pesticide s, fertilizers and adulteration and exploring the scope for better 
nature human kind environmental balance;  

80 
 
xxiii. Impetus to export promotion and supply chain management of herbal and medicinal 
plants so as to increase its production;  
xxiv. Strategic steps to complete Global Traditional Medicine Centre in Jamnagar, Gujarat;  
xxv. Conduct of Yoga practice regularly in each primary school and there should be Yoga 
teacher in each primary school; and  
xxvi. Availability of testing facilities to measure the occurrence of reported st eroids/metal 
content in Ayush products. 
(Para 4.1 ibid)

AIIA 
AIIA plays a pivotal role in enhancing India's global economic standing through its multifaceted 
approach, and significantly contributes to India's economic growth by promoting Ayurveda. 
These efforts align with the nation's broader objectives of sustainable development, innovation, 
and economic diversification. 

86 
 
• AIIA‘s integrated holistic health care model through 200 bedded hospital with 45 
specialty OPDs, and producing skilled professionals, helps reducing the healthcare costs for all 
and the burden on conventional healthcare infrastructure covering the large rural population. 
• AIIA established one satellite centre at Goa which has 100 bedded hospital and it is 
promoting medical tourism as per one of its mandate, with the support of Ministry of Ayush. 
• AIIA extended its OPD servic es by establishing 6 extension centres at various places 
including LBSNAA-Mussoorie; Supreme court, Safdarjung Hospital, NCI Jhajjar, IIT Delhi in 
New Delhi and Ekta Nagar -Gujarat covering the highest level of govt officers, judges, as well as 
cancer patie nts etc. AIIA also expanded from phase I to phase II and generate economy by 
recruiting faculties, medical staff, nursing staff, researchers, hospital staff, other technicians and 
other support staff.  
• Through organizing regular medical camps, Public Hea lth Initiatives (PHI) projects and 
DhanvantariRath, help in improving health in rural areas, and front -line workers like Police 
personals and covering their families too. 
• AIIA organises regular CMEs for the doctors and other training programs for the 
faculties, scholars and hospital staff for their advancements and upgradation of skills, in 
Ayurveda, yoga therapy, and wellness tourism. This, ultimately reflects in their services as time 
bound manner and more efficiencies towards duties.  
• AIIA fosters in ternational collaborations through MoUs with foreign universities) for 
research and Ayurveda trainings, AIIA helps in promoting and establishing Ayurveda, export of 
medicines, therapies, and wellness tourism across the globe. Till date 18 MoUs in 12 countr ies 
have been signed by AIIA under the directions of Minsitry of Ayush. Two international Research 
projects are under process on Ashwagandha in long covid with LSHTM, Uk and Understanding 
molecular mechanism of Guduchyadi yoga in Covid with Fiz, Germany. S everal other projects 
are in pipelines covering Insomnia, oral cancer, mental disorders etc.  
• AIIA promotes standardized Ayurvedic products, enabling Indian companies to tap into 
the global wellness and herbal medicine market. Indirectly, also helps in p romoting cultivation of 
medicinal plants and AYUSH -integrated farming, giving farmers higher incomes and linking 
agriculture with pharma and wellness industries. 
• AIIA-iCAINE supports the development of AYUSH -based startups through incubation 
and R&D supp ort. Focus areas include Ayurvedic nutraceuticals, herbal textiles, and point -of-
care health devices, which can become high -growth sectors and generate job -rich economic 
activity. 
To track the misleading/ objectionable advertisements, an objective has been  framed under the 
Pharmacovigilance program of ASU & H Drugs under the AOGUSY Scheme, Ministry of 
Ayush, New Delhi. The program is working through a three -tier network of a National 
Pharmacovigilance Centre (NPvCC), Five Intermediary Pharmacovigilance Cent res (IPvCs) and 

87 
 
99 Peripheral Pharmacovigilance Centres (PPvCs) established across the country. Through this 
channel of Pharmacovigilance centres objectionable/misleading advertisements are regularly 
reported concern authorities for initiating action again st the defaulters. However, more stringent 
action needs to be taken against the defaulters.

Drug Policy Section 
ii. Pharmacovigilance Program for Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani, and Homoeopathy (ASU & H) 
Drugs has been established under the Central Sector Scheme of Ministry of Ayush (AOGUSY 
Scheme).  A three tier structure functional since 2018 comprising of a National 
Pharmacovigilance Co -ordination Centre (NPvCC), Five Intermediary Pharmacovigilance 
Centres (IPvCs) and Ninety -nine Peripheral Pharmacovigilance Centres (PPvCs) is established 
across the country. All India Institute of Ayurveda (AIIA), New Delhi under Ministry of Ayush is 
the National Pharmacovigilance Co -ordination Centre (NPvCC) for the implementation of the 
National Pharmacovigilance program for Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani & Homoeopathy drugs. The 
vision of the program is to improve patient safety in Indian population by monitoring the drug 
safety in ASU & H drugs by inculcating the reporting culture of Suspected Adverse Drug 
Reactions, keeping surveillance of misleading advertisements appearing in print and electronic 
media also the awareness events are being regularly or ganized across the country to generate 
awareness regarding the AYUSH therapeutic approaches and educate about the systematic use of 
AYUSH drugs in healthcare professionals. 
 
● Ministry of Ayush issued an advisory in accordance with letter no. K -11023/01/2012-
DCC (Ayush) dated December 8, 2023, which clarifies that the permissible limits of 
heavy metals do not apply to Ayurvedic, Siddha, or Unani formulations where such 
metals are utilized as ingredients or in the processing of the formulation. This advisory i s 
pertinent and clarifies the regulatory framework in accordance with Section 3(a) of the 
Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940.  
As prescribed in Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 and Rules made thereunder, enforcement of the 
legal provisions pertaining to Quality Co ntrol and issuance of drug license of Ayurveda, Siddha, 
Unani and Homoeopathy drugs, is vested with the State Drug Controllers/ State Licensing 
Authorities appointed by the concerned State/ Union Territory Government. Rule 158 -B in the 
Drugs Rules, 1945 pr ovides the regulatory guidelines for issue of license to manufacture 
Ayurvedic, Siddha, Unani medicines and Rule 85 (A to I) in the Drugs Rules, 1945 provides the 
regulatory guidelines for issue of license to manufacture Homoeopathy medicines. It is 
mandatory for the manufacturers to adhere to the prescribed requirements for licensing of 
manufacturing units & medicines including proof of safety & effectiveness, compliance with the 
Good Manufacturing Practices (GMP) as per Schedule T & Schedule M -I for Ayurveda, Siddha, 
Unani drugs and Homoeopathy drugs respectively of the Drugs Rules, 1945 and quality standards 
of drugs given in the respective pharmacopoeia.  
Moreover, Drug Inspectors collect medicine samples regularly from manufacturing firms or sale 
shops with in their jurisdiction and sent it to Drug Testing Laboratory under Drug Control 
department for quality testing and if any sample found to be Not of Standard Quality, appropriate 
action initiates such as preventing the sale of the products from the mar ket and appropriate legal 
actions as per Drugs and Cosmetics Act 1940 and Rules made thereunder.

3.26.2 The Committee appreciates the work being carrie d out by various autonomous 
bodies and research councils under AYUSH in response to the concerns raised by the 
Members of the Committee. The Committee, however, recommends that the Ministry of 
AYUSH undertake comprehensive public awareness campaigns to elu cidate the authentic 
benefits of Ayurveda, while simultaneously implementing stringent measures to address 
quackery and misleading advertisements, particularly those purporting rapid cures for 
critical ailments such as cancer and chronic kidney disease. Th e introduction of robust 
penal provisions is imperative to deter deceptive claims that compromise the credibility of 
AYUSH systems. Furthermore, the Committee emphasizes the need to extend Yoga 
awareness initiatives beyond the observance of World Yoga Day,  ensuring sustained 
outreach at rural and block levels throughout the year. Priority should also be accorded to 
the establishment of AYUSH institutes  and Yoga Ashrams in underdeveloped states

including Bihar, Jharkhand, and Uttar Pradesh. Adequate budgetar y provisions must be 
allocated for clinical trials and scientific validation centers to bolster evidence -based 
research and promote medical tourism in regions abundant in medicinal plants, such as 
Kerala, Karnataka, and the Northeast.

AIIA 
AIIA plays a pivot al role in enhancing India's global economic standing through its multifaceted 
approach, and significantly contributes to India's economic growth by promoting Ayurveda. 
These efforts align with the nation's broader objectives of sustainable development, in novation, 
and economic diversification. 
• AIIA‘s integrated holistic health care model through 200 bedded hospital with 45 
specialty OPDs, and producing skilled professionals, helps reducing the healthcare costs for all 
and the burden on conventional healthcare infrastructure covering the large rural population. 
• AIIA established one satellite centre at Goa which has 100 bedded hospital and it is 
promoting medical tourism as per one of its mandate, with the support of Ministry of Ayush. 
• AIIA extended its  OPD services by establishing 6 extension centres at various places 
including LBSNAA-Mussoorie; Supreme court, Safdarjung Hospital, NCI Jhajjar, IIT Delhi in 
New Delhi and Ekta Nagar -Gujarat covering the highest level of govt officers, judges, as well as 
cancer patients etc. AIIA also expanded from phase I to phase II and generate economy by 
recruiting faculties, medical staff, nursing staff, researchers, hospital staff, other technicians and 
other support staff.  
• Through organizing regular medical camps,  Public Health Initiatives (PHI) projects and 
DhanvantariRath, help in improving health in rural areas, and front -line workers like Police 
personals and covering their families too. 
• AIIA organises regular CMEs for the doctors and other training programs for the 
faculties, scholars and hospital staff for their advancements and upgradation of skills, in 
Ayurveda, yoga therapy, and wellness tourism. This, ultimately reflects in their services as time 
bound manner and more efficiencies towards duties.  
• AIIA fosters international collaborations through MoUs with foreign universities) for 
research and Ayurveda trainings, AIIA helps in promoting and establishing Ayurveda, export of 
medicines, therapies, and wellness tourism across the globe. Till date 18 MoUs i n 12 countries 
have been signed by AIIA under the directions of Minsitry of Ayush. Two international Research 
projects are under process on Ashwagandha in long covid with LSHTM, Uk and Understanding 
molecular mechanism of Guduchyadi yoga in Covid with Fiz,  Germany. Several other projects 
are in pipelines covering Insomnia, oral cancer, mental disorders etc.  
• AIIA promotes standardized Ayurvedic products, enabling Indian companies to tap into 
the global wellness and herbal medicine market. Indirectly, also  helps in promoting cultivation of 
medicinal plants and AYUSH -integrated farming, giving farmers higher incomes and linking 
agriculture with pharma and wellness industries. 
• AIIA-iCAINE supports the development of AYUSH -based startups through incubation 
and R&D support. Focus areas include Ayurvedic nutraceuticals, herbal textiles, and point -of-

98 
 
care health devices, which can become high -growth sectors and generate job -rich economic 
activity.

Pharmacovigilance program of ASU & H Drugs under the AOGUSY Scheme, Ministry of 
Ayush, New Delhi. The program is working through a three -tier network of a National 
Pharmacovigilance Centre (NPvCC), Five Intermediary Pharmacovig ilance Centres (IPvCs) and 
99 Peripheral Pharmacovigilance Centres (PPvCs) established across the country. Through this 
channel of Pharmacovigilance centres objectionable/misleading advertisements are regularly 
reported concern authorities for initiating a ction against the defaulters. However, more stringent 
action needs to be taken against the defaulters.

STATUS OF UTILIZATION CERTIFICATES 
3.28 The Committee understands that Utilization Certificate (UC) in government systems is a 
crucial document that verifies h ow funds allocated for a specific project or scheme have been 
spent. Moreover, UC ensures transparency and accountability by demonstrating that the fund 
allocation has been used for its intended purpose and not misappropriated, and that the project 
achieved its desired outcomes. In this light the Committee has observed and recommended time 
and again regarding the delay in clearing pending UCs. The Committee expresses concern over 
pending UCs to number of 1549 involving Rs. 1608.29 during 2023 -24 and a total  number of 
1521 pending UCs involving an amount to the tune of Rs. 1765.85 in 2024 -25. The Committee 
observes that the largest share in pending UCs is that of NAM with 897 UCs worth Rs 1592.09 
crores. Timely submission of UCs is very crucial in scheme moni toring, subsequent release of 
funds and successful implementation of the scheme. The Committee, therefore, strongly 
recommends that the Ministry must expedite its efforts in liquidating UCs as per standard 
timeline set for the activity. States/UTs may be p ersuaded and encouraged to use the NAM portal 
efficiently for the submitting UCs, SAAPs etc. 
(Para 4.2.2 ibid)  
Action Taken 
3.28.1 Budget Section 

101 
 
The various efforts to liquidate the outstanding UCs are going on continuously in the Ministry. 
The status of Pending UCs is being reviewed on weekly basis by Secretary (Ayush) in the Senior 
Officers‘ Meeting. The status is also being reviewed/ monitored by CCA by conducting the 
meetings with the concerned Pay & Accounts offices and programme divisions of Ministr y of 
Ayush. 
Scheme Section 
Noted. 
NMPB Section 
NMPB, Ministry of Ayush has taken following efforts to expedite Pending UC‘s:  
● For early liquidation of pending UC‘s, the programme divisions of NMPB are regularly 
following up with the project implementing ag encies (State Forest Departments) and also 
hand holding of PI‘s of the projects to submit the correct UC related documents. 
● Time to time D.O. letters from CEO NMPB level are also sent to Head of concerned 
departments (PCCF) / project implementing agencies. 
● Review Meetings were conducted with PI/ Co-PI for the project for liquidation of pending 
Utilization Certificate (UC) in virtual mode (VC). 
NAM Section 
UCs submitted by State/UT Governments is examined by Ministry very thoroughly in all 
respects, specifically whether it is utilized for the same purpose for which it was sanctioned.   
Further, to obtain/liquidate UCs, the Ministry is in regular touch with States/UTs and even 
officials are also being sent to States/UTs for the same. The Central team visiting the States for 
reviewing NAM activities or other purpose are also flagging any issues of UCs invariably in all 
the meetings.  
As per conditions of Dept. of Expenditure funds are released each States/UTs only after receiving 
of 75% of their UCs. 
Due to the efforts of Ministry, UCs for an amount of Rs. 474.53 Crore has been liquidated in 
2024-25.  
Further, Ministry is continuously encouraging States/UTs to submit UCs through NAM portal. 
Further Recommendation  
3.28.2 The Committee is deeply concerned that onl y Rs. 475.00 crore worth of Utilization 
Certificates (UCs) have been liquidated in 2024 -25, which is grossly insufficient given the 
backlog of over 1,500 pending UCs under the National AYUSH Mission (NAM). Given the 

102 
 
critical role UCs play in ensuring trans parency, accountability, and effective scheme 
implementation, the mounting issue of pending liquidation of UCs should be a matter of 
concern for the Ministry. This shortcoming hampers timely monitoring and subsequent 
fund releases, adversely affecting proj ect outcomes. The Committee, therefore, urges the 
Ministry to intensify efforts to expedite the clearance of pending UCs, particularly under 
the National AYUSH Mission, to meet prescribed timelines. The Committee also 
recommends that the Ministry set a target to limit pending UCs below 10% of the total UCs 
at any given time and eventually it shall move towards nil pendency.   The Ministry should 
strengthen coordination with States and UTs, incentivize timely submissions, and promote 
optimal use of the NAM p ortal to ensure prompt and complete liquidation of UCs, thereby 
safeguarding the integrity and success of AYUSH schemes. The Committee may be 
apprised of the updated status of UCs for the year 2023-24 and 2024-25. 
 
UNIFORM &STANDARD LICENSING 
3.29 The Committee is of view that the Ministry must have a standard licensing process based on 
uniform parameters across all the States so that drug formulations do not vary from State to 
State. A standardized approach will eliminate variations in drug formulations fr om state to state, 
thereby enhancing the quality, safety, and efficacy of AYUSH medicines. This will prevent 
discrepancies in ingredient composition, dosage, and therapeutic claims, ensuring that AYUSH 
drugs meet the same regulatory and scientific benchmar ks nationwide. It will also strengthen 
consumer confidence by guaranteeing that all licensed AYUSH drugs adhere to nationally 
recognized quality standards, ultimately fostering wider acceptance and integration of AYUSH 
medicines in mainstream healthcare. 
(Para 4.3.3 ibid)  
Action Taken  
3.29.1 Drug Policy Section 
Noted. Ministry of Ayush is working further in coordination with Ayush Grid and state licensing 
Authority, Gujrat to make e -AUSHADHI portal more user friendly and robust for online 
licensing of Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani and Homoeopathy drugs. 
Further Recommendation 
3.29.2 The Committee appreciates the role of Ministry of Ayush in pushing e -AUSHADHI 
Portal more user friendly and robust for online licensing of Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani and 
Homeopathy Drugs, however, the action so take would ensure transparency in issuance of 
licencing process. The Committee‟s main emphasis is on evolving a standard licensing 
process based on uniform parameters across all states so that drug formulations do not 
vary from St ate to State, thereby enhancing the quality, safety and efficacy of Ayush 

103 
 
Medicine. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Ministry  to strive for a nationally 
recognized quality standard that will entail consumer confidence and ultimately foster 
weather acceptance and integration of Ayush medicine in mainstream healthcare.  
3.30 The Committee, keeping in view the obscure and diverse pattern of licensing that creates 
hurdles for the manufactures for seeking license for production of Ayush products. The 
Committee therefore recommends that arrangement be made to have simplified procedure for 
seeking uniform and standard license that will ensure ease of doing business for Ayush 
manufacturers thereby seeking the objective of AtmaNirbhar Bharat and Make in India  mission. 
Moreover, the manufacturers will be encouraged to setup AYUSH industry in the country for 
production of AYUSH products that will ultimately promote AYUSH sector in domestic as well as 
the global market. 
 (Para 4.3.4 ibid) 
Action Taken 
3.30.1 Drug Policy Section 
Noted. Ministry of Ayush is working further in coordination with Ayush Grid and state licensing 
Authority, Gujrat to make e -AUSHADHI portal more user friendly and robust for online 
licensing of Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani and Homoeopathy drugs. 
Further Recommendation 
3.30.2 The Committee wanted to drive home the fact that a simplified procedure for seeking 
uniform and standard license would ensure ease of doing business for Ayush 
manufacturers, thus having an eye on achieving the core objective of „Atmanirbhar Bharat‟ 
and „Make in India‟ Mission. The Committee  recommends the Ministry to facilitate the 
manufactures willing to seek license for production of Ayush products by setting up Ayush 
industry in the country so that manufacturers of Ayush pr oducts can promote Ayush 
products in domestic as well as the international markets.   
  
Clinical Trials Registry- India (CTRI) 
3.31 The Committee is of considered view that implementing a robust Clinical Trial Management 
System (CTMS) will significantly im prove documentation, efficiency, and compliance in AYUSH 
clinical research. All the research councils, institutes and other autonomous bodies engaged in 
R&D, clinic trials and related activities should be brought onto to this single platform. 
Automation of  key trial processes, including patient recruitment, randomization, monitoring, 
adverse event reporting, and regulatory submissions, will enhance efficiency. By automating 
workflows, it will reduce manual errors, enhance regulatory transparency, and accele rate trial 
approvals. Standardizing research protocols with ICMR/GCP guidelines will ensure uniformity 
across AYUSH disciplines, strengthening the scientific validation of traditional medicine. AI -

104 
 
driven analytics will support data -driven decision -making, enabling better tracking of adverse 
events and treatment outcomes. Furthermore, a well -integrated CTMS will allow multicenter 
trials, facilitating global acceptance of AYUSH -based interventions and positioning India as a 
leader in integrative medicine research. 
 (Para 4.4.1 ibid) 
Action Taken  
3.31.1 Ayurveda Section 
● NISR 
Point noted. 
● CCRAS 
The Council acknowledges the Committee‘s recommendation regarding the implementation of a 
robust Clinical Trial Management System (CTMS) and concurs with its potential t o strengthen 
the clinical research ecosystem in the Ayush sector. 
At present, the data of clinical research projects conducted by CCRAS are recorded in structured 
Case Record Forms (CRFs) and, e -CRFs. These mechanisms enable systematic data capture and 
provide restricted access to safeguard patient confidentiality and data integrity, but does not 
support advanced automation or analytics capabilities. 
Institutes can develop project -specific CTMS, adaptable to the specific needs of each type of 
project 
Siddha & Unani Section 
● CCRS 
● A robust Clinical Trial Management System (CTMS) for Ayush (Ayurveda, Yoga & 
Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, and Homeopathy) systems is essential for improving R&D 
outcomes. An effective CTMS in Ayush will be of importance for integrating  traditional 
knowledge to current scientific validation, increasing research outcomes and making Ayush -
based interventions universally acceptable and scalable. A CTMS assures systematic 
documentation, protocol compliance, and data integrity in preparation for regulatory 
clearances. A CTMS ensures real -time data capture, validation and safe storage while 
decreasing human error and bias. It aids in automating critical trial processes such as patient 
recruiting, randomization, monitoring, and reporting in successful manner. 
● Hence well -integrated CTMS research approaches and vibrant ecosystem for multi -
organizational collaborations, to expand the horizons of research in Ayush through strategic, 
coherent, and coordinated research and evidence-based translation. 

105 
 
● Recently CCRS has signed MoU with AIIMS Rishikesh for Cancer Management,

Homoeopathy Section 
The Committee‘s suggestio n has been noted.  Further, Indian Council of Medical Research 
(ICMR) has published an addendum to the ICMR National Ethical Guidelines for Biomedical 
and Health Research Involving Human Participants (2017) to provide a structured ethical 
framework for Rese arch in Integrative Medicine (RIM). As per the addendum, RIM refers to 
research involving multimodal interventions, wherein one or more Ayush practices, approaches, 
or modalities are integrated alongside those of modern/ conventional medicine to address th e 
health needs of individual(s). 
Further Recommendation 
3.31.2 The Committee is in agreement with the submission of CCRS that an effective CTMS 
in Ayush will be of importance for integrating traditional knowledge to current scientific 
validation, increasin g research outcomes and making Ayush -based interventions 
universally acceptable and scalable. A CTMS assures systematic documentation, protocol 
compliance, and data integrity in preparation for regulatory clearances. A CTMS ensures 
real-time data capture, validation and safe storage while decreasing human error and bias. 
It aids in automating critical trial processes such as patient recruiting, randomization, 
monitoring, and reporting in a successful manner. Since, a well -integrated CTMS will allow 
multicenter trials, facilitating global acceptance of AYUSH -based interventions and 
positioning India as a leader in integrative medicine research, the Committee recommends 
the Ministry of Ayush to encourage all its autonomous bodies and research councils to 
bring-forth clinical trials and related activities onto single platform i.e. Clinical Trial 
Management System (CTMS) which would significantly improve documentation efficiency 
and mandatory compliance in Ayush clinical research.

STATUS OF AYUSH EDUCATION 
3.32 The Committee notes the submission of the Ministry regarding the physical performance in 
field of AYUSH education, however, the Committee feels that the Ayush Education System has 
still require miles to march to play a level playing performance with allopathic system of 
medicine. The Committee recommends that soft skills among Ayush practitioners should be 
incorporated into the syllabus of all components of AYUSH. This should aim at instilling highest 
degree of responsibility discipline and virtue in delivering healthcare to the masses. This would 
in turn lead to increased in the Indian Systems of Medicine and would slowly and surely do away 
with the skepticism towards the alternative medicines. The Committee also recommends 
Integration of technology i n the Ayush curriculum for modernization and creating technology 
enabled work force. 
(Para 4.6.3 ibid)

disciplinary knowledge content (electives), research methodology and training enhance skill (skill 
lab and advance clinical training). This knowledge has been incorporated in the syllabus which 
will give him conf idence and a sense of responsibility and discipline in delivering healthcare to 
the masses.

methodology, and skill enhancement in the syllabus. However, the Action Taken lacks

Cooperative and Collaborative Approach between Ayush and Allopathy 
3.36 The issues relating to integrative approaches and differences of opinion on public health, 
medical education and research, policy formulation and imp lementation etc. between traditional 
medicine and modern medicine have come up before the Committee time and again. The 
Committee is of the view that these differences affect the implementation of health welfare 
schemes and policies. The Committee therefor e feels that there should be an institutional 
mechanism to bring the two systems of medicines on a common platform. The Committee desires 
that through this institutional mechanism, regular joint meetings between the M/o Ayush and M/o 
Health & Family Welfare, NMC and NCISM, NCH and Ayush Research Councils may be held to 
settle the grey areas and resolve the issues affecting health policy formulation and 
implementation strategies. The Committee strongly recommends that instead of antagonistic 
relationship, there should be cooperative and collaborative approach and cohesive equilibrium 
between the Ayush and Allopathy so that integrated and coordinated efforts may be made for 
treatment of various diseases. 
(Para 4.13 ibid)

Promotion of Yoga and Naturopathy 
3.37 The role of Yoga and Naturopathy in health promotion and preventive health is undeniable. 
Awareness of Yoga and its significance has reached international dimensions as evident in the 
celebrations of International Day of Yoga and Yoga parvs/camps being orga nized everywhere in 
the country. The Committee, however, observes that there is an unequal distribution of national 
institutes of Yoga and Naturopathy across the States and UTs, particularly in rural and 
backward areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends  that national institutes for Yoga and 
Naturopathy may be opened in states like Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand and in NER. The 
Committee further recommends that Yoga camps may be organized not just for jubilation but be 
organized regularly at Block and vi llage levels with the real intention to imbibe attitude and 
commitment to Yoga and Naturopathy by heart. The Committee also desires that Yoga classes 
may be held mandatorily in all primary schools and Yoga instructors in each school may also be 
appointed for this purpose. 
(Para 4.14 ibid) 
Action Taken  
3.37.1 Yoga & Naturopathy Section 
The Ministry of Ayush appreciates the recommendations of the Committee regarding the 
promotion of Yoga and Naturopathy for preventive and promotive health. The Ministry is 
actively engaged in promoting Yoga and Naturopathy through various initiatives and 
programmes across the country. 
The suggestion for establishment of National Institutes of Yoga and Naturopathy in States such 
as Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand, and the North  Eastern Region may be carried out 
by respective State Governments, subject to availability of resources and other relevant 
considerations. 
Further, the recommendations regarding the inclusion of Yoga classes in primary schools and 
appointment of Yoga inst ructors pertain to the Department of School Education & Literacy, 
Ministry of Education, and State Governments under whose purview primary schools function. 
 

115 
 
Further Recommendation  
 
3.37.2 The Committee is concerned that the Ministry of Ayush has only sta ted that  it is 
promoting Yoga and Naturopathy nationwide  through various initiatives and programmes 
across the country. However, the response reveals significant shortcomings as the Ministry 
of Ayush hasn‟t mentioned the details of such initiatives and p rogrammes specifically in 
regard to naturopathy. The establishment of National  Ayush Institutes in underserved 
states such as Bihar,  Odisha, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand and the North Eastern Region has 
been deferred to State Governments without a concrete central plan or support mechanism, 
risking continued regional disparities. Similarly, the integration of Yoga classes and 
instructors in primary schools is delegated to the Ministry of Education and State 
Governments, with no clear inter -ministerial coordination outlined. To address these gaps, 
the Committee recommends that the Ministry of AYUSH to actively collaborate with 
Department of School Education and relevant stakeholders in various states to facilitate 
and support the establishment of institutes and t he mandatory inclusion of Yoga education 
in schools, particularly in underserved areas. 
 
Equitable distribution of Ayush Centres 
3.38 The Committee has been apprised that there is unequal distribution of Ayush healthcare in 
the country. There are states li ke Punjab and Maharashtra where there is relatively less 
development of integrated Ayush hospitals and functional Ayush Centres. The Committee would 
like to know how many Ayush Centres and Ayush Grams are functional in all states/UTs and 
recommends that th e Ministry of Ayush must ensure equitable development of Ayush healthcare 
and ecosystem across the geographical area of the country, including setting up of Ayush Centre 
at the places where these centres are in scarce, keeping in view the region -specific requirements 
and potential in Ayush industry. The Committee has also been apprised of the coming proposals 
for 50 bedded hospitals in every district and 100 bedded hospitals in all tier -2 cities. The 
Committee expects the Ministry to expedite the process en suring priority is given to areas where 
the AYUSH healthcare is less developed. 
(Para 4.15 ibid)

executed fully. The Budgetary allocations must be aligned with both long -term objectives 
and immediate priorities, ensuring optimal fund utilization through a robust and adaptive 
monitoring mechanism. Immediate actions should include the initiation of pilot projects 
and awareness campaigns at the regional level, aiming for measurable outcomes within 6-12 
months. To mitigate deviations, the Ministry should establish real -time monitoring systems 
and mechanisms for timely course correction. F urthermore, research initiatives should be 
expedited, particularly focusing on the clinical validation of AYUSH practices. Enhanced 
inter-departmental coordination is crucial for integrating AYUSH into broader national 
health strategies and fostering international collaboration. 
3.40 As deposed by the Secretary, M/o Ayush before the Committee, the Ayush manufacturing

the rapid increase in the size of the Indian Ayush industry, with budgetary allocation of 0.012 
percent of GDP, the Committee is quite apprehensive of the ambitions of the ministry to grow 
and develop Ayush a s per its expectations. The Committee is of the view that the Ministry of 
Ayush needs to come up with more new schemes and strategies for growth and development of

Ayush activities and claim for a much more enhanced budgetary allocation in comparison to th e

research and Ayurveda trainings , AIIA helps in promoting and establishing Ayurveda, export of 
medicines, therapies, and wellness tourism across the globe. Till date 18 MoUs in 12 countries 
have been signed by AIIA under the directions of Minsitry of Ayush. Two international Research 
projects are under process on Ashwagandha in long covid with LSHTM, Uk and Understanding 
molecular mechanism of Guduchyadi yoga in Covid with Fiz, Germany. Several other projects 
are in pipelines covering Insomnia, oral cancer, mental disorders etc.  

119 
 
• AIIA promotes standardized Ayurvedic products, enabling Indian companies to tap into 
the global wellness and herbal medicine market. Indirectly, also helps in promoting cultivation of 
medicinal plants and AYUSH -integrated farming, giving farmers higher incomes a nd linking 
agriculture with pharma and wellness industries. 
• AIIA-iCAINE supports the development of AYUSH -based startups through incubation 
and R&D support. Focus areas include Ayurvedic nutraceuticals, herbal textiles, and point -of-
care health devices, which can become high -growth sectors and generate job -rich economic 
activity. 
To track the misleading/ objectionable advertisements, an objective has been framed under the 
Pharmacovigilance program of ASU & H Drugs under the AOGUSY Scheme, Ministry of 
Ayush, New Delhi. The program is working through a three -tier network of a National 
Pharmacovigilance Centre (NPvCC), Five Intermediary Pharmacovigilance Centres (IPvCs) and 
99 Peripheral Pharmacovigilance Centres (PPvCs) established across the country. Throug h this 
channel of Pharmacovigilance centres objectionable/misleading advertisements are regularly 
reported concern authorities for initiating action against the defaulters. However, more stringent 
action needs to be taken against the defaulters. 
Ayurveda is becoming popular in many countries and AIIA is contributing to the promotion of 
the traditional Ayurvedic system of medicine globally by signing MoUs and agreements with 
reputed foreign organisations 
1. AIIA is publishing three journals wherein research art icles are published, which also help 
in popularizing Ayurveda globally.  
2. Development of Benchmarks, viz; WHO benchmarks for the practice of Ayurveda 
3. Associating WHO in various programs and strategies development related to WHO - 
Global Traditional Medicine Centre and Digital Health 
4. Research Database development 
5. Protocol development 
6. Treatment guidelines development 
7. Course curriculum development for foreign Ayurveda academic programs 
8. Contribute in achieving sustainable development goals 
 
Contributing global health by international clinical research studies 
CCRAS 
Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences - is carrying out evidence based research by 
carrying out AYUSH research and standardization of medical system through application of 
AYUSH in clinica l health care, biochemistry, lifestyle diseases. The council is also involved in 
promotion and propagation of Ayurveda at national and international level. There are plans to 
expand the field of research in future by making full use of the funds and additi onal budget 
allocated to the council in the past years. This indicates working towards more increased 
budgetary allocation in future as compared to the Department of Health and Family Welfare. 

120 
 
Further Recommendation  
3.40.2 The Committee acknowledges and a ppreciates the commendable efforts of AIIA and 
CCRAS in advancing AYUSH through clinical services, international collaborations, 
research initiatives, capacity building, and public health programs. However, considering 
the Ministry of AYUSH encompasses mul tiple institutions and autonomous bodies, the 
Committee seeks clarification on whether the onus of these initiatives will rest exclusively 
with AIIA and CCRAS. Additionally, while the Ministry has articulated an ambition to 
realize a market capitalization of USD 200 billion for AYUSH within next five years, the 
Committee urges the submission of a detailed, year -wise strategic plan outlining 
implementation frameworks, institutional responsibilities, and resource allocation. A 
comprehensive response with deta ils of schemes and plans for growth and development of 
Ayush activities and the Ministry should project a much more enhanced budgetary 
allocation. 
 
FUTURE PLANS 
3.41 The Committee appreciates the larger vision of Ayush @2047 and the future plans of 
making AYUSH a multi -billion dollar market, yet the Committee is wary about the present 
situation of the healthcare in the country and observes that yearly short term goals be given a 
priority and direction to provide best AYUSH healthcare to the public by establ ishing 
PHCs/CHCs at district level etc. The Committee is in agreement of the Ministry of Ayush that the 
target of 30 lakh Ayush professionals in country by 2047, setting up one Ayush HQ in every 
district with at least one Ayush College, creation of over 50  lakh new jobs, increasing 
contribution of Ayush to up to 7.7% of GDP etc. by the 2047 is achievable by converting the long 
duration ambitious targets into short -term plan with smaller objective & goals and having co -
ordinating and controlling monitoring m echanism over the implementation progress of 
projects/schemes/programmes ensuring achievement of set targets/goals/objectives. The 
Committee, in this regard, recommends the Ministry to make concerted efforts to implement the 
projects/schemes/programme through development of botanical drugs, entering into Free Trade 
Agreements (FTA) with favourable countries for enlarging global markets & enhancing exports 
level to a new height, establishing global hub of traditional medicine alongside WHO GTMC, 
production &  promotion standard & quality drugs, ensuring widespread availability of 
AyushAushadi Kendra etc. 
(Para 5.2.1 ibid) 
Action Taken  
3.41.1 IC Section 
The recommendation of the committee has noted and Ministry of Ayush requested to Ministry of 
Commerce and Industry to include Ayush product related matter in bilateral agreement.

CDN Section 
Drug Policy Section 
Taken into consideration. A Central Sector Scheme — AYUSH Drugs Quality and Productivity 
Enhancement Scheme (AOGUSY) — has been implemented. The first co mponent of the scheme 
is: 
Strengthening and upgradation of AYUSH pharmacies and drug testing laboratories to achieve 
higher standards. The objective of this component is to bring Ayurveda, Siddha, Unani and 
Homeopathy (ASU&H) pharmacies and drug testing la boratories (DTL) up to World Health 
Organisation (WHO) Good Manufacturing Practices (GMP) and National Accreditation Board 
for Testing and Calibration Laboratories (NABL) standards. 
CCRAS

in the field of evidence based medicine through standardization and clinical use of Ayurvedic

cancer patients etc. AIIA also expanded from phase I to phase II and generate economy by 
recruiting faculties, medical staff, nursing staff, researchers, hospital staff,  other technicians and 
other support staff.

It is welcome step to acknowledge the current state of healthcare in the country and observes that

122 
 
remote areas and to provide health to all it is suggested to strengthen the local health tradition s

Further Recommendation  
3.41.2 The Committee appreciates the bilateral agreement of the Ministry of Ayush with the 
Ministry of Commerce and Industry to include Ayush products. The C ommittee however 
wants to know the steps taken by the Ministry of Ayush to establish Ayush specific 
healthcare centres in PHCs/CHCs at district level in all the states across India. The 
Committee appreciates the larger goal of the Ministry of Ayush to have  30 lakh ayush 
professionals in the country by 2047. However, to effectively realize the goal it is imperative 
on the part of the Ministry to split it into short term goals. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Ministry of Ayush come up with compr ehensive yearly plans of 
producing a minimum number of say 1 lakh Ayush Professionals each year. In extension, 
the Ministry must chalk out the plan for absorption of these professionals in appropriate 
jobs. Hence job creation is a must in Ayush Healthcare to achieve the cherished goals of 
Ayush@2047. Further, as suggested by NISR, the Committee endorses the opinion that to 
provide health to all it is suggested to strengthen the local health traditions and train the 
traditional healer in primary health care in remote areas. The Committee recommends the 
Ministry of Ayush to find out the traditional health practitioners in remotest areas of the 
country and archive their knowledge in traditional healing which can be utilized for a 
larger population of this country and beyond.  
3.42 The Committee understand that the Global Footprint of Yoga needs to be further enhanced 
to tap into the global market and globalization of Yoga and Ayurveda training under Yoga 
Certification Board, Ayurveda Training Accreditation Board (ATAB) may be promoted 
internationally for highest global participation in receiving and propagating the ancient form of 
knowledge rechristened in Ayush System i.e. Ayurveda, Yoga & Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, 
Rigpa-Sowa and Homeopathy for alternative mode of treatment of diseases and healthy living.. 
(Para 5.2.2)

NISR 
It is stated that there is a need to further enhance the presence of Yoga in  the global market and 
globalizationof Yoga and Ayurveda training should be promoted at the international level under 
the Yoga CertificationBoard, Ayurveda Training Accreditation Board (ATAB) to have the 
highest global participation. It is suggested that i nstead of only Yoga and Ayurveda entire Ayush 
systems should be promoted on global level based on unique strengths of Ayurveda, Yoga and 
Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, Rigpa-Sowa and Homeopathy. 
 
Further Recommendation  
3.42.2 The Committee observes that the Ministry of AYUSH has undertaken commendable 
initiatives to promote AYUSH systems globally, notably through the International 
Cooperation Scheme, academic collaborations, and the implementation of accredited 
training programs under the Ayurveda Training Ac creditation Board (ATAB) and Yoga 
Certification Board (YCB). The contributions of institutions such as AIIA, CCRAS and 
ITRA in disseminating and operationalizing these programs are noteworthy. However, the 
action taken does not fully align with the Committ ee‟s recommendation in letter and spirit 
to significantly enhance global participation in Yoga and Ayurveda training. However, the 
current measures appear to remain largely domestic in scope and lack a comprehensive 
international outreach framework. The Co mmittee strongly feels that there is an immense 
potential in Ayush systems on the global stage and it must be promoted on a global level 
based on unique strengths of Ayurveda, Yoga and Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, Rigpa -
Sowa and Homeopathy. It is recommende d that the Ministry develop a cohesive global 
strategy encompassing international partnerships, welcoming  international students to get 
Yoga Certification in India under aforementioned schemes. The Ministry of Ayush should 
strive to make the ayurveda insti tutes of highest international repute, that all over the 
world people would want to get education in traditional system of medicine especially in 
Ayurveda, Yoga & Naturopathy to begin with.

INFORMATION, EDUCATION AND COMMUNICATION (IEC) 
4.1 The Committee observes that the State -wise reach of Arogya Fair/Ayurveda Parv is less 
compared the size of India comprising of 28 states and 8 UTs. The  Committee recommends that 
the reach should cover 50% of the Indian states in coming year and eventually should cover all 
over India in near future to propagate the potentiality of Ayush System in prevention and 
treatment of common ailments. The Committee,  therefore, recommends the Ministry to leave no 
stone unturned in efficiently and effectively implementing IEC Scheme, thus generating 
awareness amongst the common masses especially in rural, urban slums, hilly & tribal area. 
 
 (Para 3.4.4 ibid) 
 
Action Taken   
4.1.1 IEC Section 
The Observation of the committee is noted. 
INSTITUTE OF TEACHING AND RESEARCH IN AYURVEDA (ITRA) 
 
4.2 The Committee after reviewing the performance of the Institute is of considered view that it is 
living upto the status of “Institute of National Importance” (INI). The institute should promote its 
potential on national and international level to further increase the interest of public to the 
Ayurveda. The Committee believes that ITRA can have new innovative experiments in Ayurveda 
syllabi and once such innovation proves to be successful in generation of more confident 
Ayurveda vaidyas, then that model can be implemented throughout the nation via NCISM. 
Ayurveda can further be improved by implementing newer teaching and research method ology. 
The Committee reviewed the performance of the institute in terms of students enrolled vs. 
students passed and is of the view that the pass percentage is an area that needs focus and 
institute may take necessary steps like focused group classes etc f or enhancing the pass 
percentage of students in various courses. The Committee further recommends digitizing the 
Ayurveda clinical training the Ayurveda scholars that would enhance the learning of Ayurveda 
based diagnosis treatments to a new height. 
(Para 3.11.5 ibid)

NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR HOMEOPATHY 
 
4.3 The Committee observes that against the actual expenditure of Rs. 13.37 crore in 2023 -24 an 
amount of Rs. 20.00 crore was allocated in BE 2024 -25 to NCH which was reduced to Rs. 16.50 
crore at RE stage. The allocation reduced at RE stage because of non -hiring of contractual staff 
and non -appointment of members of commission. The Committee takes into account that the 
enhancement in expenses/estimated expenses was to meet the additional requirement for hiring of 
contractual staff, new building and appointment of new members of commiss ion, creation/filling 
up of 45 regular posts. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Ministry that due 
diligence must be shown for hiring of requisite staff. Construction of new building, appointment 
of new Members of Commission and creation/fil ling up of 45 regular posts for enabling NCH to 
work to fullest capacity and achieving its set objectives and goals. 
 
(Para 3.12.2 ibid)

CENTRAL COUNCIL FOR RESEARCH IN SIDDHA 
4.5 The Committee is given to understand that CCRS is an apex body for conducting research in 
Siddha system of medicine and provides healthcare facilities, however, CCRS has been allocated 
the lowest budget as compared to other A YUSH systems in field of Medical Education Training 
and Research. The Committee views Siddha system of Medicine as an integral part of AYUSH 
and accordingly observes that siddha should be further strengthened to carry out more research 
and promote Siddha system of medicine across the country to deliver the advantages of evidence -
based Siddha medicine to the public. The Committee finds that no funds have been allocated 
under the Special Component Plan for Scheduled Castes and Tribal Area Sub -Plan for Siddha 
system, whereas for other systems of AYUSH, significant funds have been allocated. Therefore 
the Committee desires to know the reasons thereof and recommends that Special Component 
Plan for Scheduled Castes and Tribal Area Sub-Plan may also be allocated for Siddha system. 
(Para 3.17.3 ibid) 
Action Taken

The Institute has an intake capacity of 125 in UG in Jaipur and another 100 seats in its extension 
Centre of NIA, Panchkula along with 130 seats for post -graduation. Every year equal number of 
graduates and post-graduates pass out from NIA and they are utilized by Government or provide 
colleges as well as hospitals and many of them also practice in rural areas also t hereby providing 
the services of these professionals pass out from NIA for the benefit of the country in its Ayush 
curricular treatment activities. 
● NEIAH 
NEIAH, Shillong is running BAMS and BHMS programmes started from the Academic Session 
2016 -2017 onwards along with its attached hospitals at Mawdiangdiang, Shillong Meghalaya. 
NEIAH has also admitted  PG  Scholars  in Homoeopathy subjects in  the Academic 2023-2024. 

133 
 
At present internship for fourth Batch BAMS and BHMS (Academic session 2019 -2020) is 
ongoing. 
Apart from running BAMS,  BHMS and PG  programmes,a One -year Panchakarma Technician 
Certificate course with intake capacity of 20 students annually started from the academic session 
2018-2019. So far, four batches of panchakarma trainees from different North Eastern (NE) states 
have successfully completed their training. Among the trainees, three women were offered 
employment within the Institute itself, while one woman was employed by the Health 
Department of Meghalaya. 
● NISR

The National Institute of Homoeopathy (NIH) has an approved intake capacity of 126 students 
for undergraduate (UG) programs and 47 students for postgraduate (PG) programs, in accordance 
with the maximum infrastructure capacity provided. 
 
Gyan Bharatam 
4.8 The Committee takes into account that announcement of GyanBhartam Mission in Budget 
Speech- 2025 and its objectives of the Mission and hopes that the Mission will march to a 
milestone in documentation and conservation of the manuscript heritage with various resources 
that may inter -alia include academic institutions, museums, libraries and private collectors 
across the country. The Committee is of the considered view that setting up of National Digital 
Repository of Indian Knowledge System would pave the way for dissemination of hidden treasury 
of Indian past knowledge of Indian System of Medicine to the global forefront and world would 
know about the past glory of alternate mode of medicine for treatment of diseases through Shalya 
and Shalakya, Sushruta and Patanjali. 
(Para 4.8.1 ibid)

Ayush Grid 
4.9 The Committee feels that the Ayush GRID can be a game changer in maki ng available the 
HarGharAyuryog and other schemes and initiatives of AYUSH to larger public. The Committee 
welcomes the AI chatbots etc. as envisaged under Ayush GRID and suggests that chatbot 
interactions regarding medication etc. should be moderated to p reliminary checks and basic 
prescriptions. Moreover, Ayush Grid, being the IT backbone of entire Ayush Sector would lead to 
integration of all stakeholders entailing the benefits of co -operation and collaboration amongst 
all stakeholders, thereby generatin g professional pursuit and thus accelerating promotion of 
Ayush Sector as a whole. 
(Para 4.9.1 ibid) 
Action Taken  
4.9.1 
Ayush Grid Section 
The Ayush Grid is in the process of the development of various utility and assistance chatbots 
under the emerging te chnology component. The interactions regarding medication would be 
guided by the experts/physicians and basic details on the consultations will be provided by the AI 
system that the physician can get guidance from. The Ayush Grid applications shall help pr omote 
the Ayush sector as a whole in a transparent and holistic manner using the emerging 
technologies. 
 
Ayush Vision @2047 
 4.10 The Committee, after perusal of the goals, strategies and roadmap of the Ministry for 
reaching the goal of Ayush@2047, the Com mittee observes that the Ministry is on the right 
course of action. The setting up of GTMC, increase in export of medicinal plants, setting up of 
12273 plus AAMs etc. would enrich Ayush Eco -system. The Committee desires that the Ministry 
of Ayush should leave no stone unturned in achieving the set targets set in Ayush Vision @2047. 
The Committee desires the work done so far by the Ministry in line with the larger vision of the 
Ministry. The Committee, however, observes with concern a general trend in under -utilization of 
the allocated funds to the Ministry for various schemes that may halt progress of Ayush Mission. 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry enhance the monitoring mechanism in place for 
overseeing the optimal utilization of the funds allocat ed. The Committee desires the Ministry to 
be more effective in timely utilization of budgetary allocation. The Committee is of considered 
view that timely development of infrastructure and capital assets will put in place a strong 
foundation for the growth of AYUSH. 
(Para 5.1.1 ibid)

Action Taken 
4.10.1 CDN Section 
 
International Cooperation 
The Government of India signed the Host Country Agreement for the establishment of the WHO 
World Centre for Traditional Medicine in Jamnagar, Gujarat on March 25, 2022.  It is the first 
such centre in a developing country. The Prime Minister of India, Shri Narendra Modi, laid the 
foundation stone of the WHO -GTMC in the presence of DrTedrosAdhanom Ghebreyesus, 
Director-General of WHO. The Centre is a collaborative project of the Ministry of AYUSH, 
India and the World Health Organization (WHO) and is the world‘s first and only global centre 
for traditional medicine. The Donor Agreement has been signed between WHO and the 
Government of India (Ministry of AYUSH) in July 2024 t o determine the financial terms for the 
implementation of the activities of the WHO World Centre for Traditional Medicine in Jamnagar, 
Gujarat. The WHO GTMC was established with the vision to accelerate the convergence of 
ancient wisdom and modern science towards the holistic health and well -being of people and the 
planet.
